### RollPacker: Mitigating Long-Tail Rollouts for Fast, Synchronous RL Post-Training
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21009v1)
- **Authors**: Wei Gao, Yuheng Zhao, Dakai An, Tianyuan Wu, Lunxi Cao, Shaopan Xiong, Ju Huang, Weixun Wang, Siran Yang, Wenbo Su, Jiamang Wang, Lin Qu, Bo Zheng, Wei Wang
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning (RL) is a pivotal post-training technique for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, synchronous RL post-training often suffers from significant GPU underutilization, referred to as bubbles, caused by imbalanced response lengths within rollout steps. Many RL systems attempt to alleviate this problem by relaxing synchronization, but this can compromise training accuracy. In this paper, we introduce tail batching, a novel rollout scheduling strategy for synchronous RL that systematically consolidates prompts leading to long-tail responses into a small subset of rollout steps (long rounds), while ensuring that the majority of steps (short rounds) involve only balanced, short rollouts. By excluding long responses from short rounds and rescheduling them into a few designated long rounds, tail batching effectively reduces GPU idle time during rollouts and significantly accelerates RL training without sacrificing accuracy. We present RollPacker, a system that fully harnesses the benefits of tail batching through holistic optimizations across all three RL stages: elastic parallelism adaptation for rollout, dynamic resource allocation and scheduling for reward, and stream-based training. Empirical results show that RollPacker achieves a 2.03x-2.56x end-to-end training time reduction compared to veRL and up to 2.24x speedup compared to RLHFuse for the Qwen2.5 family of LLMs on up to 128 H800 GPUs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "RollPacker: Mitigating Long-Tail Rollouts for Fast, Synchronous RL Post-Training":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of GPU underutilization ("bubbles") in synchronous Reinforcement Learning (RL) post-training of Large Language Models (LLMs) caused by imbalanced response lengths (long-tail rollouts).  It introduces "tail batching," a novel rollout scheduling strategy that consolidates prompts generating long responses into dedicated "long rounds," while keeping the majority of steps ("short rounds") composed of balanced, short responses. The paper then presents RollPacker, a system implementing tail batching with holistic optimizations across rollout, reward, and training stages, namely dynamic tensor parallelism (TP), a reward scheduler, and a stream trainer. Empirical results show significant end-to-end training time reductions compared to existing RL systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of tail batching is reasonably novel within the specific context of RL post-training for LLMs.  While batch reordering and scheduling techniques exist more broadly in machine learning, applying it explicitly to the long-tail problem in *synchronous* RL rollout phases is a differentiating factor. The combination of tail batching with the three system-level optimizations further enhances its novelty. The tailored speculative execution approach during short rounds also adds to the innovative aspects.

*   **Significance:** Reducing GPU underutilization in RL post-training is significant, as it directly translates to faster training times and lower costs for LLM development. RL post-training is a pivotal approach for improving LLM performance, therefore, any technique that improves its efficiency holds potential to impact downstream applications. The reported speedups (2x-2.5x) are substantial and, if reproducible, represent a worthwhile contribution to the field. However, the paper would benefit from demonstrating the impact on downstream LLM performance (e.g., how much accuracy can be gained in the same amount of training time).

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Definition:** Clearly articulates a relevant and pressing problem.
    *   **Technical Approach:** Well-defined technical components with clear implementation details and rationale. The design choices are justified with thorough analysis (e.g., why dynamic TP is needed, why asynchronous reward computation is advantageous).
    *   **Empirical Evaluation:**  Comprehensive evaluation against strong baselines and on large language models (Qwen2.5 family), demonstrating consistent improvements. The breakdown of speedup contributions from different optimizations provides valuable insights.
    *   **System-Level Optimizations:** Addresses bottlenecks across the entire RL pipeline, rather than focusing on just one stage.
    *   The emphasis on maintaining synchronous training guarantees is an advantage over alternative methods that introduce off-policy training.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** RollPacker is a complex system with numerous interacting components. This complexity might make it difficult to reproduce or adapt to different RL settings.
    *   **Limited Generalization:** The evaluation is primarily focused on the Qwen2.5 family of LLMs and specific datasets. It's not immediately clear how well RollPacker would generalize to other LLM architectures or RL tasks.
    *   **Impact on LLM Performance:** The paper focuses primarily on training *speed*. A crucial aspect is whether faster training leads to better model performance, or whether it merely accelerates training to the same level of performance. The validation accuracy plots offer some evidence of preservation, but lack a clear comparison on test data. A more thorough investigation here would be beneficial.
    *   **Justification of Hyperparameters:** The choice of certain hyperparameters (e.g., in the adaptive timeout mechanism, the percentage threshold for GPUs to scale down) could be better justified or investigated.

*   **Potential Influence:** RollPacker's techniques, particularly tail batching and the dynamic TP adjustment, could inspire further research on efficient RL post-training methods.  The system-level optimization approach serves as a good example for future work.  If open-sourced, the code could be valuable for practitioners looking to accelerate their LLM training pipelines.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

RollPacker presents a well-engineered system for addressing a significant bottleneck in LLM RL post-training. The tail batching concept is novel and the empirical results demonstrate substantial performance gains. However, the complexity of the system and the limited evaluation (especially regarding generalization to different LLMs and a more in-depth analysis of the downstream performance) temper enthusiasm. While the individual optimizations might build upon existing techniques, the combination and application within this specific context is meaningful. This score reflects the significance of the problem addressed, the effectiveness of the proposed solution, and the reasonable evidence provided, while acknowledging the potential for improved evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Automatic Red Teaming LLM-based Agents with Model Context Protocol Tools
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21011v1)
- **Authors**: Ping He, Changjiang Li, Binbin Zhao, Tianyu Du, Shouling Ji
- **Abstract**: The remarkable capability of large language models (LLMs) has led to the wide application of LLM-based agents in various domains. To standardize interactions between LLM-based agents and their environments, model context protocol (MCP) tools have become the de facto standard and are now widely integrated into these agents. However, the incorporation of MCP tools introduces the risk of tool poisoning attacks, which can manipulate the behavior of LLM-based agents. Although previous studies have identified such vulnerabilities, their red teaming approaches have largely remained at the proof-of-concept stage, leaving the automatic and systematic red teaming of LLM-based agents under the MCP tool poisoning paradigm an open question. To bridge this gap, we propose AutoMalTool, an automated red teaming framework for LLM-based agents by generating malicious MCP tools. Our extensive evaluation shows that AutoMalTool effectively generates malicious MCP tools capable of manipulating the behavior of mainstream LLM-based agents while evading current detection mechanisms, thereby revealing new security risks in these agents.
- **Summary**: Here's a concise summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Automatic Red Teaming LLM-based Agents with Model Context Protocol Tools" addresses the security risks associated with using Model Context Protocol (MCP) tools in LLM-based agents. MCP tools standardize interactions between agents and external resources, but they also introduce vulnerabilities to tool poisoning attacks. The authors propose AUTOMALTOOL, an automated red teaming framework that generates malicious MCP tools. It works by modifying the descriptions of MCP tools within benign MCP server packages to achieve attacker-specified goals, evade detection, and preserve code functionality.  The framework uses a multi-agent system comprising an Initial Generator, Oracle, Effectiveness Evaluator, and Tool Optimizer. The authors evaluate AUTOMALTOOL on two widely used LLM-based agents, Claude Desktop and Cline, using various backend LLMs and benign MCP server packages. Their results demonstrate the framework's effectiveness in generating malicious MCP tools capable of manipulating agent behavior while evading existing detection mechanisms. The paper highlights new security risks associated with MCP tools in LLM-based agents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper presents a novel approach to red teaming LLM-based agents, specifically targeting the vulnerabilities introduced by MCP tools. While the concept of tool poisoning isn't entirely new, the automated framework for generating malicious MCP tools and systematically evaluating their impact on agent behavior is a significant contribution. The idea of a multi-agent system dedicated to generating evasive, functional, and targeted malicious tools is innovative.

*   **Significance:** The work has significant implications for the security of LLM-based agents.  The widespread adoption of MCP tools makes this vulnerability a practical and pressing concern. By demonstrating the feasibility of tool poisoning attacks and the limitations of existing detection methods, the paper raises awareness about the need for more robust security measures. Furthermore, the AUTOMALTOOL framework can be used to proactively identify and mitigate vulnerabilities in LLM-based agent systems and to develop more robust detection strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Automation:** The key strength is the automation aspect of the red teaming process.  Previous efforts have largely been manual or proof-of-concept.
    *   **Evasion:**  The framework is designed to create evasive malicious tools, which makes the evaluation more realistic and highlights the limitations of current defenses.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:**  The evaluation is reasonably comprehensive, using multiple LLM-based agents, MCP servers, and detection methods. The metrics used are appropriate for measuring the effectiveness and evasiveness of the generated malicious tools.
    *   **Addresses key challenges:** The paper explicitly identifies and tackles the challenges of diverse tool functionalities, evasion of detection, and maintaining code functionality.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Attacks:** The paper primarily focuses on two types of malicious behaviors: incorrect parameter invocation and output results misinterpretation. While important, this is not an exhaustive list of potential attack vectors.  More diverse attack scenarios could be considered.
    *   **Black-box Assumption:** The assumption of a black-box scenario (no knowledge of the target LLM-based agent's internal details) is a simplification.  In real-world settings, attackers may have some level of knowledge about the system, which could influence their attack strategies.
    *   **Simulated Environment:** The Effectiveness Evaluator uses a simulated LLM-based agent environment. While useful for initial evaluation, this may not fully capture the complexities and nuances of real-world agent behavior.  The results might not perfectly translate to all LLM-based agent architectures.
    *   **Dependence on LLMs:** The framework relies heavily on LLMs for generating malicious tools and evaluating their effectiveness.  The performance of AUTOMALTOOL is thus dependent on the capabilities of the underlying LLMs, which are constantly evolving.  The framework's effectiveness may vary as LLMs improve or as new vulnerabilities are discovered.
    *   **Limited Ethical Discussion**: The paper touches upon ethical concerns but could benefit from a more detailed discussion regarding the responsible use and potential misuse of the tool. Clear guidelines on data sharing and usage should be outlined.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research on the security of LLM-based agents.  It provides a valuable framework for red teaming and identifying vulnerabilities in MCP systems. It also highlights the need for more robust detection methods and defense mechanisms. Furthermore, this paper can be used by the developers of MCP-based LLMs to better construct robust safety nets and increase agent robustness.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a strong contribution to the field by presenting a practical and automated framework for red teaming LLM-based agents. It addresses a relevant and growing security concern related to the use of MCP tools. The framework is well-designed and the evaluation is reasonably thorough. However, the limitations related to the scope of attacks, the black-box assumption, and the simulated environment prevent it from achieving a higher score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Predicting LLM Reasoning Performance with Small Proxy Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21013v1)
- **Authors**: Woosung Koh, Juyoung Suk, Sungjun Han, Se-Young Yun, Jay Shin
- **Abstract**: Given the prohibitive cost of pre-training large language models, it is essential to leverage smaller proxy models to optimize datasets before scaling up. However, this approach becomes challenging for reasoning capabilities, which exhibit emergent behavior that only appear reliably at larger model sizes, often exceeding 7B parameters. To address this, we introduce rBridge, showing that small proxies ($\leq$1B) can effectively predict large-model reasoning by aligning more closely with (1) the pre-training objective and (2) the target task. rBridge achieves this by weighting negative log-likelihood with task alignment, using reasoning traces from frontier models as gold labels. In our experiments, rBridge (i) reduces dataset ranking costs by over 100x relative to the best baseline, (ii) achieves the strongest correlation across six reasoning benchmarks at 1B to 32B scale, and (iii) zero-shot transfers predictive relationships across pre-training datasets at 1B to 7B scale. These findings indicate that rBridge offers a practical path for exploring reasoning-oriented pre-training at lower cost.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RBRIDGE, a method for predicting the performance of large language models (LLMs) with reasoning capabilities using smaller proxy models.  It addresses the challenge that reasoning abilities emerge reliably only in larger models (7B+ parameters), making dataset optimization for reasoning computationally expensive.  RBRIDGE improves the correlation between small proxy model performance and large model reasoning by: (1) aligning the proxy model's training objective with the target task via weighting the negative log-likelihood loss using reasoning traces generated by a larger "frontier" model as gold labels, and (2) token-level task alignment by dynamically weighting the negative log-likelihood loss for each token based on its task relevance inferred from the frontier model's confidence. Experiments demonstrate that RBRIDGE effectively reduces dataset ranking costs, achieves strong correlation between proxy (1B) and target models (up to 32B), and enables zero-shot transfer of predictive relationships across datasets.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of using reasoning traces from frontier models to weight the loss function of smaller proxy models is novel. The token-level task alignment adds another layer of sophistication. The paper successfully bridges the gap between small and large models by carefully considering how the training objective is aligned with the target task, a problem often overlooked in simpler scaling law analyses.  The zero-shot transfer capability is also a significant contribution.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in LLM research: the high cost of pre-training large models and the difficulty of optimizing datasets for reasoning. RBRIDGE offers a practical and cost-effective solution for exploring reasoning-oriented pre-training at lower cost. The potential impact on dataset curation and model development is significant. The findings enable cheaper experimentation and more efficient resource allocation in the development of reasoning-capable LLMs.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-motivated problem:** The paper clearly identifies a significant challenge in LLM development.
    * **Novel and well-defined method:** RBRIDGE is a carefully designed and implemented method.
    * **Comprehensive experiments:** The paper presents a thorough empirical evaluation, including dataset ranking, proxy-target correlation analysis, and zero-shot transfer experiments.  The ablations clearly show the contribution of the different parts of RBRIDGE.
    * **Clear and well-written:** The paper is easy to understand and follow.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on "frontier" models:** RBRIDGE relies on having access to a larger "frontier" model to generate reasoning traces.  This might limit its applicability in scenarios where such models are not available or are prohibitively expensive to run, at least initially. Although, the authors have addressed this to a certain extent by claiming low-cost operation of the frontier models, and promising the open-sourcing of a precomputed data set.
    * **Scalability to more complex reasoning:**  The paper primarily focuses on benchmarks solvable through chain-of-thought reasoning.  It's unclear how well RBRIDGE would generalize to more complex reasoning tasks requiring more intricate intermediate steps and knowledge integration.
    * **Focus on scaling pre-training data size:** The experiments mostly involve scaling the pre-training dataset sizes. There's not much detail on different *types* of pre-training data, which are often considered a different factor that can be used to improve performance.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to be influential in the field, providing a valuable tool for dataset optimization and model development, but also inspiring future research on similar problems in related scenarios.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**  The paper introduces a novel and practical method that addresses an important problem in LLM research. It provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims and has the potential to significantly reduce the cost of developing reasoning-capable LLMs. The weaknesses mainly stem from the assumptions of having access to a frontier model, and a narrow focus on chain-of-thought benchmarks. Also, there's a lack of focus on scaling pre-training *data types*.  Despite the weaknesses, the demonstrated improvements and its practical implications justify a relatively high score.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Actor-Critic without Actor
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21022v1)
- **Authors**: Donghyeon Ki, Hee-Jun Ahn, Kyungyoon Kim, Byung-Jun Lee
- **Abstract**: Actor-critic methods constitute a central paradigm in reinforcement learning (RL), coupling policy evaluation with policy improvement. While effective across many domains, these methods rely on separate actor and critic networks, which makes training vulnerable to architectural decisions and hyperparameter tuning. Such complexity limits their scalability in settings that require large function approximators. Recently, diffusion models have recently been proposed as expressive policies that capture multi-modal behaviors and improve exploration, but they introduce additional design choices and computational burdens, hindering efficient deployment. We introduce Actor-Critic without Actor (ACA), a lightweight framework that eliminates the explicit actor network and instead generates actions directly from the gradient field of a noise-level critic. This design removes the algorithmic and computational overhead of actor training while keeping policy improvement tightly aligned with the critic's latest value estimates. Moreover, ACA retains the ability to capture diverse, multi-modal behaviors without relying on diffusion-based actors, combining simplicity with expressiveness. Through extensive experiments on standard online RL benchmarks,ACA achieves more favorable learning curves and competitive performance compared to both standard actor-critic and state-of-the-art diffusion-based methods, providing a simple yet powerful solution for online RL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Actor-Critic without Actor (ACA), a novel reinforcement learning (RL) framework that eliminates the explicit actor network present in traditional actor-critic methods. ACA leverages the gradient field of a noise-level critic to directly generate actions through a critic-guided denoising process. This approach simplifies the architecture, reduces computational overhead, and ensures closer alignment between policy improvement and value estimates. ACA maintains the ability to capture diverse, multi-modal behaviors without relying on diffusion-based actors. Extensive experiments on standard online RL benchmarks demonstrate that ACA achieves favorable learning curves and competitive performance compared to standard actor-critic and state-of-the-art diffusion-based methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of eliminating the explicit actor and directly deriving actions from the critic's gradient field is relatively novel in the context of online RL. Existing diffusion-based RL methods typically utilize a separate diffusion model as the actor.  Reinterpreting the guided denoising as an implicit actor is a clever and potentially impactful insight. The combination of this implicit actor with a noise-level critic, explicitly conditioned on the diffusion timestep, adds to the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance stems from the increased simplicity, parameter efficiency, and improved training stability offered by ACA. By removing the actor network, ACA tackles the challenges of actor-critic training, such as hyperparameter tuning and architectural decisions related to the actor. The experimental results, showcasing competitive performance with fewer parameters compared to existing methods, strengthen the claim of improved efficiency. Further, the demonstrated ability to maintain multi-modality without explicit diffusion-based actors also adds to the impact. The results convincingly demonstrate the approach’s effectiveness in standard online RL settings.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Architectural Simplicity:** The elimination of the explicit actor is a significant simplification.
    *   **Parameter Efficiency:** The paper demonstrates competitive performance with fewer parameters.
    *   **Improved Training Stability:** By directly coupling policy improvement to the critic, ACA reduces policy lag and potentially increases training stability. This aligns with empirical findings presented in the paper.
    *   **Multi-Modality:** Retains the capacity for multimodal action exploration without an explicit actor.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments cover a range of MuJoCo tasks and comparisons with relevant baselines. Ablations provide useful insights into the impact of key hyperparameters.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost of Denoising:**  The paper acknowledges that iterative denoising during training can be more computationally expensive than simpler action sampling methods like those in SAC. The paper claims that it’s still more parameter-efficient, but computational efficiency is an important factor and should be investigated more thoroughly. Also, the necessity of fixed guidance weights, while commonly used, calls for future adaptive methods.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Analysis:** While the method is empirically validated, a deeper theoretical analysis would be beneficial.  More formal justifications for the convergence properties and benefits of the noise-level critic would strengthen the work.  Proposition 1, while helpful, is a starting point.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments focus on relatively standard MuJoCo benchmarks. Evaluating ACA on more complex, high-dimensional environments, or in tasks with sparse rewards, would further demonstrate its scalability and robustness.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   ACA could inspire further research into actor-free RL algorithms.
    *   The concept of using a critic-guided denoising process to generate actions could be applied to other RL settings.
    *   The parameter-efficient design could be particularly useful in domains with limited computational resources.

**Rigorous Rationale:**
ACA presents a solid contribution to the field of reinforcement learning. It introduces a conceptually elegant and empirically validated method for improving actor-critic algorithms. However, it needs further validation across environments (complex) and a more concrete analysis of computation costs associated with denoising versus other more simple methods. Given ACA presents a new paradigm and achieves competitive performance with fewer parameters in comparison to diffusion-based and actor-critic baselines, while exhibiting some limitations, a score that reflects ACA's novelty is suitable.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### KeyWorld: Key Frame Reasoning Enables Effective and Efficient World Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21027v1)
- **Authors**: Sibo Li, Qianyue Hao, Yu Shang, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Robotic world models are a promising paradigm for forecasting future environment states, yet their inference speed and the physical plausibility of generated trajectories remain critical bottlenecks, limiting their real-world applications. This stems from the redundancy of the prevailing frame-to-frame generation approach, where the model conducts costly computation on similar frames, as well as neglecting the semantic importance of key transitions. To address this inefficiency, we propose KeyWorld, a framework that improves text-conditioned robotic world models by concentrating transformers computation on a few semantic key frames while employing a lightweight convolutional model to fill the intermediate frames. Specifically, KeyWorld first identifies significant transitions by iteratively simplifying the robot's motion trajectories, obtaining the ground truth key frames. Then, a DiT model is trained to reason and generate these physically meaningful key frames from textual task descriptions. Finally, a lightweight interpolator efficiently reconstructs the full video by inpainting all intermediate frames. Evaluations on the LIBERO benchmark demonstrate that KeyWorld achieves a 5.68$\times$ acceleration compared to the frame-to-frame generation baseline, and focusing on the motion-aware key frames further contributes to the physical validity of the generated videos, especially on complex tasks. Our approach highlights a practical path toward deploying world models in real-time robotic control and other domains requiring both efficient and effective world models. Code is released at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/Keyworld-E43D.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "KeyWorld," a framework to improve the efficiency and effectiveness of text-conditioned robotic world models.  Instead of generating every frame in a video sequence (a frame-to-frame approach), KeyWorld focuses on generating a sparse set of keyframes representing significant motion transitions.  It employs the Ramer-Douglas-Peucker (RDP) algorithm to identify these keyframes from robot pose trajectories.  A Diffusion Transformer (DiT) model is then trained to generate these keyframes from textual task descriptions and initial observations. A lightweight Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) interpolates the intermediate frames.  The authors demonstrate that KeyWorld achieves significant speedups compared to frame-to-frame generation while maintaining or improving video quality and physical plausibility in the LIBERO benchmark.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Improved Efficiency:** The core idea of focusing computation on keyframes is a logical and practical approach to addressing the computational burden of world models. The paper demonstrates substantial speedups (up to 5.68x) compared to the frame-to-frame baseline, which is a significant achievement for real-time robotics applications.
    *   **Enhanced Physical Plausibility:** The motion-aware keyframe selection using RDP appears to be effective in capturing essential dynamics. This is supported by the improved object-level accuracy and qualitative results, where KeyWorld better models the robot's interaction with target objects.
    *   **Modular Design:** The framework is modular, separating keyframe generation and frame interpolation, allowing for flexibility and potential for improvement in each component independently.
    *   **Clear Evaluation:** The paper uses appropriate metrics (PSNR, SSIM, object-level accuracy) and evaluates KeyWorld on a well-established benchmark (LIBERO). The ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of each component.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** Addresses a critical bottleneck that limits the wider adoption of world models in real-time robotic control.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on CogVideoX:** The keyframe generator relies heavily on CogVideoX. While finetuning is performed, the fundamental architecture is borrowed. This reduces the novelty of the overall approach, as much of the image generation capability comes from the pre-trained model.
    *   **Limited Keyframe Generation Architecture Innovation:** The core keyframe generation task is accomplished with a DiT architecture, which, while strong, doesn't seem to have significant modifications or optimizations tailored for this specific sparse generation objective.
    *   **Limited Ablation:** It would have been interesting to see a more comprehensive ablation study on the keyframe selection algorithm itself. How does the performance vary with different parameters of the RDP algorithm, and how does RDP compare to other potential keyframe selection methods?
    *   **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** The evaluation is confined to the LIBERO dataset. While LIBERO is a good benchmark, it is important to discuss the potential limitations and challenges of applying KeyWorld to other robotic domains or with different types of sensors. For example, the RDP algorithm may not be suitable for all motion patterns.
    *   **Incremental Novelty:** The novelty primarily lies in the combination of existing techniques (RDP, DiT, CNN interpolation) into a coherent framework for efficient robotic world modeling. This is more of an engineering contribution than a theoretical breakthrough.
    *   **Gap Estimation Approach:** While efficient, the gap estimator design seems simplistic and may lead to suboptimal performance on more complex videos, limiting the potential for further performance enhancements to interpolation.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper offers a practical and effective method for accelerating robotic world models. The speedups achieved are compelling, and the improved physical plausibility is valuable.
    *   The framework provides a good starting point for further research on keyframe-based world modeling, potentially inspiring the development of more sophisticated keyframe selection and generation techniques.
    *   By making world models more computationally efficient, the paper contributes to their wider adoption in real-world robotic applications.

**Score and Justification:**

I would assign this paper a **Score: 6.5**.

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a sound engineering solution to a practical problem. It clearly demonstrates the effectiveness of its approach in terms of both speed and quality. However, the reliance on existing models (CogVideoX, FILM) for generation and interpolation reduces the overall novelty. While the integration of these components with the RDP-based keyframe selection is clever, the algorithmic novelty is limited. Furthermore, the lack of extensive ablation studies and generalizability discussion prevents a higher evaluation. The contribution is incremental, but the practical implications are non-negligible, warranting this score.

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 6/10

### Who Gets Cited Most? Benchmarking Long-Context Language Models on Scientific Articles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21028v1)
- **Authors**: Miao Li, Alexander Gurung, Irina Saparina, Mirella Lapata
- **Abstract**: This paper introduces SciTrek, a novel question-answering benchmark designed to evaluate the long-context reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs) using scientific articles. Current long-context benchmarks often rely on non-scientific texts, focus on simple information retrieval tasks, or employ artificial contexts. SciTrek addresses these limitations by proposing complex questions that require information aggregation and synthesis across multiple full-text scientific articles. Questions and their ground-truth answers are automatically generated by formulating them as SQL queries over a database constructed from article metadata (titles, authors, and references). The SQL operations provide explicit, verifiable reasoning steps for fine-grained error analysis, and the construction process scales to contexts up to 1M tokens with minimal supervision. Extensive experiments on a diverse set of open-weight and proprietary LLMs demonstrate that SciTrek poses a significant challenge as the context length increases, with supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning offering only limited gains. Our analysis reveals systematic shortcomings in models' abilities to perform basic numerical operations and accurately locate specific information in long contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Who Gets Cited Most? Benchmarking Long-Context Language Models on Scientific Articles":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SciTrek, a new question-answering benchmark designed to evaluate the long-context reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) using scientific articles. SciTrek addresses limitations in existing long-context benchmarks by: (1) using scientific articles, (2) requiring information aggregation and synthesis across multiple articles, and (3) generating questions and ground-truth answers automatically by formulating them as SQL queries over a database of article metadata (titles, authors, references).  The SQL formulation provides verifiable reasoning steps for error analysis, and the construction process is scalable to 1M+ token contexts. The authors conduct experiments with various open-weight and proprietary LLMs, demonstrating that SciTrek presents a significant challenge, with limited gains from supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL).  The analysis highlights model shortcomings in numerical operations and locating specific information within long contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates strong novelty in several aspects. The key innovation is the automatic generation of QA pairs based on SQL queries derived from scientific article metadata. This provides a scalable way to create complex, multi-document reasoning tasks with explicitly defined reasoning steps, setting it apart from many existing benchmarks. Using scientific articles is also an improvement over generic long-context benchmarks.

*   **Significance:** The benchmark addresses a critical need in evaluating LLMs for scientific applications. The ability to synthesize information across multiple documents is a core requirement for tasks like literature review and research synthesis. By highlighting the limitations of current LLMs in this context, SciTrek can drive further research into improving long-context reasoning in scientific domains. The explicit SQL structure for question generation allows for more granular error analysis, helping to pinpoint specific weaknesses in model architectures and training strategies.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Scalability:** The automated question generation process is a major strength, allowing for easy scaling to larger contexts and datasets with minimal human supervision.
    *   **Explicit Reasoning Steps:** Formulating questions as SQL queries provides clear and verifiable reasoning paths, enabling in-depth error analysis.
    *   **Relevance to Scientific Tasks:** The benchmark focuses on realistic scientific tasks, making it highly relevant for evaluating LLMs designed for research applications.
    *   **Thorough Evaluation:**  The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of multiple LLMs, both open-weight and proprietary, revealing their limitations in the context of SciTrek.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Superficial Questions:** As the authors admit, the questions are based on metadata and do not deeply engage with the *content* of the articles. This means the benchmark primarily assesses retrieval and basic aggregation rather than more sophisticated scientific reasoning.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Mitigation Strategies:** While the paper explores SFT and RL, the gains are modest. Further investigation into architectural modifications or training techniques specifically tailored to address the identified shortcomings would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Reliance on Specific Metadata:**  The benchmark's design is tied to metadata easily extractable from Semantic Scholar. While this ensures scalability, it might limit the variety of questions that can be generated compared to a manually curated benchmark.

*   **Potential Influence:** SciTrek has the potential to become a valuable tool for evaluating and improving LLMs for scientific tasks. Its scalability and explicit reasoning steps make it well-suited for identifying specific areas where models struggle. By providing a more challenging and realistic benchmark, SciTrek can encourage research into developing more robust long-context reasoning abilities in LLMs. The fact that it highlights limitations even in state-of-the-art models underscores its value.

**Overall:**

SciTrek is a well-designed and valuable contribution to the field of long-context evaluation. While the questions are limited in depth, the scalable generation process, explicit reasoning steps, and focus on scientific articles make it a significant improvement over many existing benchmarks. The analysis clearly demonstrates the challenges faced by LLMs in synthesizing information from multiple scientific articles, highlighting the need for further research in this area.
**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 8/10

### FORCE: Transferable Visual Jailbreaking Attacks via Feature Over-Reliance CorrEction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21029v1)
- **Authors**: Runqi Lin, Alasdair Paren, Suqin Yuan, Muyang Li, Philip Torr, Adel Bibi, Tongliang Liu
- **Abstract**: The integration of new modalities enhances the capabilities of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) but also introduces additional vulnerabilities. In particular, simple visual jailbreaking attacks can manipulate open-source MLLMs more readily than sophisticated textual attacks. However, these underdeveloped attacks exhibit extremely limited cross-model transferability, failing to reliably identify vulnerabilities in closed-source MLLMs. In this work, we analyse the loss landscape of these jailbreaking attacks and find that the generated attacks tend to reside in high-sharpness regions, whose effectiveness is highly sensitive to even minor parameter changes during transfer. To further explain the high-sharpness localisations, we analyse their feature representations in both the intermediate layers and the spectral domain, revealing an improper reliance on narrow layer representations and semantically poor frequency components. Building on this, we propose a Feature Over-Reliance CorrEction (FORCE) method, which guides the attack to explore broader feasible regions across layer features and rescales the influence of frequency features according to their semantic content. By eliminating non-generalizable reliance on both layer and spectral features, our method discovers flattened feasible regions for visual jailbreaking attacks, thereby improving cross-model transferability. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach effectively facilitates visual red-teaming evaluations against closed-source MLLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FORCE: TRANSFERABLE VISUAL JAILBREAKING ATTACKS VIA FEATURE OVER-RELIANCE CORRECTION":

**Summary:**

This paper addresses the problem of limited transferability in optimization-based visual jailbreaking attacks against Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The authors analyze the loss landscape and feature representations of these attacks, revealing that they often reside in high-sharpness regions and rely on model-specific features in early layers and semantically poor high-frequency spectral components. To mitigate this, they propose a Feature Over-Reliance Correction (FORCE) method. FORCE includes a layer-aware regularization to encourage exploration of broader feature regions in early layers and a spectral rescaling strategy to reduce the influence of non-semantic high-frequency content.  The authors demonstrate through experiments that FORCE improves cross-model transferability across diverse MLLM architectures and datasets, suggesting a flatter loss landscape and reduced reliance on model-specific vulnerabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Novelty:*

The paper introduces a novel perspective on the failure of visual jailbreaking attacks to transfer across different MLLM architectures. By analyzing the loss landscape and feature representations, the authors identify specific causes of this failure, specifically the over-reliance on model-specific features and high frequency noise components, This diagnostic approach itself contributes a valuable insight to the community. The FORCE method, by correcting these over-reliances, contributes a tangible improvement to the transferability of jailbreaking attacks. While some components, like spectral masking, have been used previously in other adversarial contexts, their combination with layer-wise feature analysis and regularization within the MLLM jailbreaking domain represents a novel application.

*Significance:*

The issue of limited transferability is a significant barrier to effectively evaluating the robustness of closed-source MLLMs through visual red-teaming. Improving transferability allows for the discovery of more generalizable vulnerabilities, which is essential for developing robust safety mechanisms. FORCE offers a practical approach to improve this transferability, making it a valuable tool for researchers and practitioners involved in MLLM safety and security. The potential influence of this work lies in its ability to enable more effective red-teaming evaluations, ultimately leading to safer and more reliable MLLM deployments.

*Strengths:*

*   **Clear Problem Definition and Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of limited transferability and its importance for MLLM security.
*   **Rigorous Analysis:** The authors provide a detailed analysis of the loss landscape and feature representations, offering compelling evidence for their claims.
*   **Novel Method:** The FORCE method is well-motivated and addresses the identified issues effectively.
*   **Extensive Experiments:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results across diverse MLLMs and datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of FORCE.
*   **Well-Written and Organized:** The paper is easy to follow and presents its ideas in a clear and concise manner.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Limited Real-World Evaluation:** While the paper demonstrates improved transferability across various MLLMs, more rigorous evaluation in real-world scenarios, perhaps with human evaluators, would strengthen the findings.
*   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The paper could benefit from a more thorough discussion of the sensitivity of FORCE to different hyperparameter settings, potentially including an ablation study on those settings.
*   **Computational Overhead:** The FORCE method introduces additional computational overhead due to the layer-aware regularization and spectral rescaling components. A discussion of the computational cost and potential optimizations would be beneficial.
*   **Scope of the Attack:** The paper focuses primarily on a specific class of visual jailbreaking attacks (optimization-based). It is unclear how well FORCE would generalize to other types of visual or multimodal attacks.

*Justification for Score:*

The paper addresses a critical and timely problem in the field of MLLM security. The novelty lies in the methodical identification of the causes of limited transferability in visual jailbreaking attacks and the development of the FORCE method. The comprehensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of FORCE, paving the way for more effective red-teaming evaluations. While the weaknesses mentioned above (real-world evaluation, hyperparameter sensitivity, computational cost, and scope) limit the impact somewhat, the paper still represents a significant contribution to the field.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### SupCLAP: Controlling Optimization Trajectory Drift in Audio-Text Contrastive Learning with Support Vector Regularization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21033v1)
- **Authors**: Jiehui Luo, Yuguo Yin, Yuxin Xie, Jinghan Ru, Xianwei Zhuang, Minghua He, Aofan Liu, Zihan Xiong, Dongchao Yang
- **Abstract**: Contrastive language-audio pretraining, which aims to unify multimodal representations in a shared embedding space, serves as a cornerstone for building a wide range of applications, from cross-modal retrieval to cutting-edge multimodal large language models. However, we find that the perpendicular component of the pushing force from negative samples in contrastive learning is a double-edged sword: it contains rich supplementary information from negative samples, yet its unconstrained nature causes optimization trajectory drift and training instability. To address this, we propose Support Vector Regularization (SVR), a method that introduces an auxiliary support vector to control this perpendicular component, aiming to harness its rich information while mitigating the associated trajectory drift. The efficacy of SVR is critically governed by its semantic radius, for which we explore two unsupervised modeling strategies: direct parameterization and an adaptive radius predictor module enhanced with constraints to improve its predicting accuracy. Extensive experimental results demonstrate that our method surpasses widely used baselines like InfoNCE and SigLIP loss across classification, monolingual retrieval, and multilingual retrieval on standard audio-text datasets. Both the theoretical analysis and the experimental results on optimizing trajectory drift validate the correctness and effectiveness of our SVR method.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and rigorous evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper identifies a problem in audio-text contrastive learning (specifically in CLAP models) that the authors term "optimization trajectory drift." They attribute this drift to the perpendicular component of the pushing force exerted by negative samples, which, while containing useful information, can destabilize training. To address this, they propose Support Vector Regularization (SVR), which introduces a new regularization term based on "support vectors" derived from the text embeddings. A semantic radius parameter is crucial to SVR's performance, and they explore two unsupervised methods for modeling this radius: a static learnable scalar and a dynamic predictor module. Extensive experiments on audio-text datasets demonstrate that their approach, SupCLAP, improves performance compared to baselines like InfoNCE and SigLIP across various tasks.  The paper also provides theoretical analysis to support their claims regarding the efficacy of SVR.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel perspective on a known problem (contrastive learning instability) by framing it as "optimization trajectory drift."  The idea of attributing this drift to the perpendicular component of the pushing force is insightful. The proposed SVR method is also novel, particularly the adaptation of support vector concepts to regularize contrastive learning in this specific way, and the approaches for modeling the semantic radius. However, the individual components (contrastive loss, regularization, etc.) are not entirely new, but the specific combination and analysis are.

* **Significance:** Addressing instability in contrastive learning is an important problem because it hinders the ability to learn robust and generalizable multimodal representations. If the proposed method effectively mitigates drift and improves alignment between audio and text, it could lead to advances in downstream tasks like cross-modal retrieval and multimodal language models. The empirical results seem to support this potential. The theoretical analysis, while not revolutionary, adds weight to the proposed method and helps to explain its efficacy.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Framing:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of optimization trajectory drift and provides a compelling explanation for its cause.
    * **Method:** The SVR method seems well-motivated and the design choices are reasonably justified.  The exploration of two different semantic radius modeling strategies enhances the practicality of the approach.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are extensive and cover a range of tasks and datasets. The ablation studies help to understand the contribution of each component.
    * **Theoretical Support:** The inclusion of a theoretical analysis strengthens the paper's claims.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Incremental Improvement:** While the experimental results show improvements over baselines, the margins may be considered incremental rather than revolutionary. This may be dependent on the datasets chosen. The gains might not justify the added complexity of the method.
    * **Unsupervised Radius Modeling:** While the unsupervised radius modeling is a clever idea, it may be a limiting factor.  A more sophisticated approach, possibly using some form of weak supervision or self-supervision, could potentially lead to further improvements. The constraint term seems like a 'patch' to deal with problems in the unsupervised approach, rather than a robust solution.
    * **Limited generalizability statement:** While the approach is sound, there is a statement to ensure there wasn't any leakage, or exploitation of LLM-generated data. This would help further validate the approach is robust.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on contrastive learning, particularly in the multimodal domain.  The concept of optimization trajectory drift could provide a useful lens for analyzing and addressing instability in other contrastive learning settings.

* **Justification of Score:** While the paper introduces a novel perspective and presents a well-designed method with strong empirical validation, the improvements are not dramatically superior to existing methods. Furthermore, more robustly-analyzing the generalizability, it is difficult to justify a very high score.
* *Score: 7*

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 7/10

### Generative AI for FFRDCs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21040v1)
- **Authors**: Arun S. Maiya
- **Abstract**: Federally funded research and development centers (FFRDCs) face text-heavy workloads, from policy documents to scientific and engineering papers, that are slow to analyze manually. We show how large language models can accelerate summarization, classification, extraction, and sense-making with only a few input-output examples. To enable use in sensitive government contexts, we apply OnPrem$.$LLM, an open-source framework for secure and flexible application of generative AI. Case studies on defense policy documents and scientific corpora, including the National Defense Authorization Act (NDAA) and National Science Foundation (NSF) Awards, demonstrate how this approach enhances oversight and strategic analysis while maintaining auditability and data sovereignty.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OnPrem.LLM, an open-source Python toolkit designed to facilitate the use of large language models (LLMs) in sensitive government settings, specifically Federally Funded Research and Development Centers (FFRDCs). It highlights the need for secure, on-premises LLM deployment due to the text-heavy workloads and data sensitivity inherent in FFRDC operations. The paper outlines the architecture of OnPrem.LLM, detailing its four main modules: LLM (for interfacing with various LLM backends), Ingest (for document processing and storage), Pipelines (for pre-built workflows like summarization and extraction), and App (a Streamlit-based web interface). The paper then presents example use cases, including concept-focused summarization, open-ended survey classification, legal analysis extraction, and question-answering with horizon scanning. Finally, it addresses potential risks associated with LLMs (hallucinations, over-reliance, data exposure) and suggests mitigation strategies, such as auditability, human-in-the-loop validation, and sovereign deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a practical solution to a pertinent problem: deploying LLMs securely and effectively within government organizations that handle sensitive information.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper's strength lies in its practical orientation. It provides a concrete toolkit (OnPrem.LLM) along with readily implementable example use cases. This contrasts with purely theoretical papers, making it immediately useful for practitioners in the field.
    *   **Open-Source Availability:** The open-source nature of OnPrem.LLM encourages adoption, collaboration, and customization, maximizing its impact.
    *   **Security Considerations:** The paper explicitly addresses security and privacy concerns, which are critical for government use. The focus on on-premises deployment and mitigation strategies for data exposure are significant.
    *   **Comprehensive Architecture:** The modular design of OnPrem.LLM, with its LLM, Ingest, Pipelines, and App modules, provides a well-rounded framework for document intelligence.
    *   **Web Interface:** The Streamlit-based web interface is also a positive aspect, as it allows non-technical users to interact with the system easily.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Technical Depth:** The paper provides a high-level overview of OnPrem.LLM but lacks a deep technical dive into the implementation details of each module. For example, the specific techniques used for OCR or table extraction are not detailed. It would be beneficial to show the LLM prompts or evaluation metrics.
    *   **Limited Evaluation:** While the paper presents example use cases, it does not include rigorous quantitative evaluations of the performance of OnPrem.LLM on these tasks. This makes it difficult to assess the effectiveness of the toolkit compared to other existing solutions.
    *   **Lack of Novelty:** The individual components of OnPrem.LLM (e.g., LLM backends, vector stores, Streamlit UI) are not novel in themselves. The primary novelty lies in the *integration* of these components into a cohesive framework tailored for FFRDCs.
    *   **Reliance on Public Data:** The use of publicly available data in the examples limits the generalizability of the results to real-world, sensitive government data.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's significance lies in its potential to accelerate the adoption of LLMs within government organizations while addressing crucial security and privacy concerns. The focus on practical use cases and the open-source nature of the toolkit make it a valuable resource for FFRDCs.
    *   The framework could potentially empower analysts, researchers, and policy makers. By doing this, it enables them to process and analyze information efficiently, and improve their decision-making capabilities.

*   **Justification for Score:**

    Considering the above evaluation, the paper showcases good practicality and usefulness. The potential impact in speeding up government operations is significant, and it addresses key ethical concerns. However, a lack of rigorous quantitative evaluation and limited novelty hold it back. As such,

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Behind RoPE: How Does Causal Mask Encode Positional Information?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21042v1)
- **Authors**: Junu Kim, Xiao Liu, Zhenghao Lin, Lei Ji, Yeyun Gong, Edward Choi
- **Abstract**: While explicit positional encodings such as RoPE are a primary source of positional information in Transformer decoders, the causal mask also provides positional information. In this work, we prove that the causal mask can induce position-dependent patterns in attention scores, even without parameters or causal dependency in the input. Our theoretical analysis indicates that the induced attention pattern tends to favor nearby query-key pairs, mirroring the behavior of common positional encodings. Empirical analysis confirms that trained models exhibit the same behavior, with learned parameters further amplifying these patterns. Notably, we found that the interaction of causal mask and RoPE distorts RoPE's relative attention score patterns into non-relative ones. We consistently observed this effect in modern large language models, suggesting the importance of considering the causal mask as a source of positional information alongside explicit positional encodings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the role of the causal mask in Transformer decoders as a source of positional information, even in the absence of explicit positional encodings like RoPE or causal dependencies in the input data. The authors theoretically prove and empirically demonstrate that the causal mask induces position-dependent patterns in attention scores, favoring nearby query-key pairs, similar to traditional positional encodings. Furthermore, they show how the causal mask interacts with RoPE, biasing its relative attention pattern towards a non-relative form. They validate these findings by analyzing trained models and simulating transformers without parameters or explicit positional encodings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in explicitly proving and demonstrating the inherent positional information encoding capability of the causal mask. While prior works have hinted at this functionality, this paper provides a more direct explanation and explores its interaction with widely used positional encodings like RoPE. The analysis of how the causal mask distorts the relative attention pattern of RoPE is another novel contribution.

*   **Significance:** The findings have implications for understanding how Transformer decoders work, especially in large language models. Recognizing the causal mask as a positional encoding mechanism provides a more complete picture of how positional information is processed. The paper highlights that when working with models with both causal masks and explicit position encodings, it is vital to consider the interactions of both components, which are often not considered independently. This insight is crucial for designing more effective and efficient architectures and improving length generalization. The results might contribute to refining positional encoding strategies and improving model performance and length generalization capabilities.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Theoretical grounding:** The theoretical proof is a significant strength, providing a formal basis for understanding the causal mask's role.
    *   **Empirical validation:**  The paper supports its theoretical claims with extensive empirical analysis using simulated transformers and real trained models.
    *   **Clear exposition:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the complex interactions between the causal mask and RoPE.
    *   **Relevance:**  The research directly addresses a fundamental aspect of Transformer decoders, making it relevant to a broad audience in the NLP and deep learning communities.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited analysis of FFNs and parameters:** While the paper demonstrates the effect of causal masks and their impact on positionality when combined with position embeddings such as ROPE, the effect of feed-forward networks on the positional dependency isn't well analyzed.
    *   **Performance impact analysis:** While the paper explains how the mask encodes positional information, it lacks a quantitative performance analysis demonstrating the impact of these findings on downstream tasks or generalization ability.
    *   **Limited exploration of alternative positional encodings:** It primarily focuses on RoPE, and a broader comparison with other positional encoding methods would strengthen the analysis.

*   **Potential Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research on positional encoding in Transformers. It suggests a more holistic view where the causal mask is considered an integral part of the positional information processing pipeline. This may lead to new architectures, training strategies, and improved length generalization techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper is well-executed and presents novel and significant findings, its impact is somewhat limited by the absence of a direct performance evaluation and the incomplete treatment of FFNs and learned parameters. The theoretical contribution is strong, and the empirical evidence supports it well. The lack of direct performance gains is a significant factor. Nevertheless, this paper has provided significant insights for understanding positional encoding within large language models.
Therefore, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Combinatorial Creativity: A New Frontier in Generalization Abilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21043v1)
- **Authors**: Samuel Schapiro, Sumuk Shashidhar, Alexi Gladstone, Jonah Black, Royce Moon, Dilek Hakkani-Tur, Lav R. Varshney
- **Abstract**: Artificial intelligence (AI) systems, and large language models (LLMs) in particular, are increasingly employed for creative tasks like scientific idea generation, constituting a form of generalization from training data unaddressed by existing conceptual frameworks. Though in many ways similar to forms of compositional generalization (CG), combinatorial creativity (CC) is an open-ended ability. Instead of evaluating for accuracy or correctness against fixed targets, which would contradict the open-ended nature of CC, we propose a theoretical framework and algorithmic task for evaluating outputs by their degrees of novelty and utility. From here, we make several important empirical contributions: (1) We obtain the first insights into the scaling behavior of creativity for LLMs. (2) We discover that, for fixed compute budgets, there exist optimal model depths and widths for creative ability. (3) We find that the ideation-execution gap, whereby LLMs excel at generating novel scientific ideas but struggle to ensure their practical feasibility, may be explained by a more fundamental novelty-utility tradeoff characteristic of creativity algorithms in general. Importantly, this tradeoff remains persistent even at scale, casting doubt on the long-term creative potential of LLMs in their current form. Together, our conceptual framework and empirical findings provide a foundation for understanding and improving creativity in modern AI models, marking a new frontier in generalization abilities.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a theoretical framework and an algorithmic task for evaluating combinatorial creativity (CC) in AI models, particularly large language models (LLMs). The framework models creativity within a conceptual space represented as a graph, where the task involves finding novel paths between concepts while adhering to logical constraints.  The paper empirically investigates the scaling behavior of creativity in decoder-only Transformers by systematically varying model size, depth, and width. Key findings include the existence of optimal model depths and widths for creative ability, and the identification of a novelty-utility tradeoff that persists even at scale, potentially explaining the ideation-execution gap observed in LLMs.  The authors argue that their work provides a foundation for understanding and improving creativity in AI, marking a new frontier in generalization abilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several strong aspects regarding novelty. The explicit framing of combinatorial creativity as a distinct generalization ability beyond compositional generalization is insightful. The theoretical framework, particularly the formalization of novelty and utility within a graph-based conceptual space, is a valuable contribution. Furthermore, the systematic empirical analysis exploring the impact of architectural choices (depth, width, number of heads) on creativity within LLMs is novel. The finding that there is an optimal depth-to-width ratio is not predictable. Finally, while the ideation-execution gap and the idea of novelty-utility trade-off have been mentioned previously, formally linking it as fundamental constraint that persists even at scale and architectural innovations offers a fresh perspective.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical gap in the understanding of creative abilities in AI. By providing a framework and algorithmic task for evaluating creativity, the research moves beyond anecdotal evidence and enables more rigorous investigation of creative performance. The identification of architectural sweet spots and the novelty-utility tradeoff offers practical guidance for designing more creative AI systems. Addressing the ideation-execution gap is particularly relevant, as it speaks to the limitations of current LLMs in translating creative ideas into practical applications. The use of utility constraints as abstractions for failure modes encountered in scientific idea generation is also interesting and could allow more focused study on this aspect.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data:** A major weakness is the reliance on synthetic data. While controlled and allowing for systematic study, the synthetic graph might oversimplify the complexity of real-world knowledge graphs and creative domains. The extent to which the findings generalize to real-world creative tasks is uncertain.
    *   **Limited Scale:**  The study's limitation to 100M parameter models is a concern. Modern LLMs are orders of magnitude larger, and it's possible that the observed trends change significantly at larger scales. The authors mention this explicitly, but it remains a substantial limitation.
    *   **Operationalization of Utility and Novelty**: The chosen metrics for utility and novelty are necessarily specific to the graph pathfinding domain. While reasonable abstractions, they might not fully capture the richness and nuances of these concepts in broader creative contexts. For instance, the measure of 'surprise of the path' might not fully account for the serendipitous nature of some creative insights.
    *   **Limited Scope of Creativity**: The study focuses solely on combinatorial creativity. While important, it neglects other forms of creativity (e.g., transformational creativity) that might be relevant for AI systems.

*   **Potential Influence:** Despite the limitations, the paper has the potential to be influential. The framework provides a solid foundation for future research on creativity in AI. The empirical findings offer valuable insights for designing architectures that are better suited for creative tasks. The identification of the novelty-utility tradeoff could stimulate research on techniques for overcoming this limitation. The use of utility levels as abstraction of failure modes is also a worthwhile area for additional exploration.

*   **Overall Assessment:**  The paper represents a significant step forward in the formal study of creativity in AI. While the limitations related to synthetic data and model scale are important, the conceptual framework, algorithmic task, and empirical findings offer valuable contributions to the field.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**
A score of 7 reflects a solid contribution with notable novelty and potential influence, tempered by significant limitations. The paper moves beyond descriptive accounts to offer a formal framework and systematic empirical study, which is a major strength. However, the reliance on synthetic data and the relatively small model scale limit the generalizability of the findings and prevent a higher score.  The insights are interesting and contribute to the conversation, but the synthetic data limits its real-world impact.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Reinforcement Learning Fine-Tuning Enhances Activation Intensity and Diversity in the Internal Circuitry of LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21044v1)
- **Authors**: Honglin Zhang, Qianyue Hao, Fengli Xu, Yong Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) acquire extensive prior knowledge through large-scale pretraining and can be further enhanced via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) or reinforcement learning (RL)-based post-training. A growing body of evidence has shown that RL fine-tuning improves the capability of LLMs beyond what SFT alone achieves. However, the underlying mechanisms why RL fine-tuning is able to enhance the capability of various LLMs with distinct intrinsic characteristics remain underexplored. In this study, we draw inspiration from prior work on edge attribution patching (EAP) to investigate the internal differences of LLMs before and after RL fine-tuning. Our analysis across multiple model families shows two robust effects of online RL post-training: (i) an overall increase in activation intensity, indicating that more internal pathways are engaged and their signals become stronger, and (ii) greater diversity in activation patterns, reflected by higher entropy and less concentrated edge distributions. These changes suggest that RL reshapes information flow to be both more redundant and more flexible, which may explain its advantage in generalization. Notably, models fine-tuned with Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) deviate from these trends, exhibiting substantially weaker or inconsistent internal changes compared to PPO- and GRPO-based training. Together, our findings provide a unified view of how RL fine-tuning systematically alters the internal circuitry of LLMs and highlight the methodological distinctions between online RL and preference-based approaches. Our code is open source at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/llm_rl_probing_analysis-F673.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how reinforcement learning (RL) fine-tuning alters the internal circuitry of large language models (LLMs). It draws inspiration from edge attribution patching (EAP) to compare LLMs before and after RL fine-tuning.  The analysis reveals that online RL post-training leads to (i) increased activation intensity, indicating more active internal pathways, and (ii) greater diversity in activation patterns, shown by higher entropy and less concentrated edge distributions.  Notably, models fine-tuned with Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) exhibit less consistent internal changes compared to PPO- and GRPO-based training. The study concludes that online RL systematically reshapes information flow within LLMs, potentially explaining its superior generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**

    * **Novelty of Approach:** The paper provides a mechanistic perspective on the benefits of RL fine-tuning for LLMs, going beyond just evaluating downstream task performance. Using EAP to analyze changes in internal activation patterns is a solid methodological choice.
    * **Comprehensive Analysis:** The study considers multiple LLM families (Deepseek-Math, Mistral, Distilled-Qwen, Qwen2.5), datasets (GSM8K, MATH, College Math), and hyperparameters, bolstering the robustness of the findings.
    * **Important Distinction:** Highlighting the differences between DPO and other RL methods like PPO/GRPO is a crucial contribution. It suggests that not all RL fine-tuning approaches are created equal and provides a direction for further investigation.
    * **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written, organized, and the figures (particularly Figure 1 explaining EAP) aids in understanding the complex methodology.
    * **Reproducibility:** Releasing the code enhances the potential for others to build upon this work.

* **Weaknesses:**

    * **Limited Scope of Analysis:** While the study analyzes *how* RL alters internal structures, it provides limited insight into *why* these changes lead to improved performance on specific tasks. It hints at better generalization but doesn't directly link specific structural changes to specific performance improvements.
    * **Reliance on Correlation:** The study largely relies on correlations. While the results are suggestive, causal relationships between specific circuit changes and improved capabilities are not firmly established. More targeted interventions (beyond ablation) might be needed.
    * **Complexity of Interpretation:** Although EAP helps, interpreting the significance of specific edge weight changes is still challenging. The jump from "increased activation intensity" to "better generalization" requires further justification and bridging arguments.
    * **Questionable metric:** The Dist. Kurt (kurtosis) is hard to interpret. What distribution of edge weights means a model should be more accurate?

* **Significance:**

    * The paper is significant because it addresses a vital question: Why does RL fine-tuning work so well? By linking performance gains to changes in internal LLM circuitry, it contributes to a more holistic understanding.
    * The comparison of DPO to other RL methods provides essential guidance to the community, suggesting that online RL is likely superior for reshaping internal dynamics.
    * The methodology (EAP) can be applied to investigate other fine-tuning techniques and architectural innovations in LLMs.

* **Potential Influence:**

    * The paper is likely to inspire further research into the mechanistic interpretability of RL-trained LLMs.
    * The findings can inform the design of more effective RL fine-tuning algorithms that better shape the internal representations of LLMs.
    * The work might motivate researchers to explore hybrid approaches that combine the benefits of online RL (PPO/GRPO) with the stability of DPO.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper offers a valuable and original contribution to the field. It moves beyond surface-level evaluations of RL fine-tuning and offers a mechanistic perspective. However, the reliance on correlations and the limited explanation of the functional consequences of circuit changes warrant a moderate score. The paper offers a good first look.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### GeoRef: Referring Expressions in Geometry via Task Formulation, Synthetic Supervision, and Reinforced MLLM-based Solutions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21050v1)
- **Authors**: Bing Liu, Wenqiang Yv, Xuzheng Yang, Shichang Wang, Junzhuo Liu, Peng Wang, Guoqing Wang, Yang Yang, Heng Tao Shen
- **Abstract**: AI-driven geometric problem solving is a complex vision-language task that requires accurate diagram interpretation, mathematical reasoning, and robust cross-modal grounding. A foundational yet underexplored capability for this task is the ability to identify and interpret geometric elements based on natural language queries. To address this, we introduce the task of Referring Expression Comprehension (REC) for geometric problems, which evaluates whether models can localize points, shapes, and spatial relations in diagrams in response to textual prompts. We present GeoRef, a benchmark dataset constructed from existing geometric problem corpora, featuring diverse, high-quality annotations and queries. Due to the lack of annotated data for this task, we generate a large-scale synthetic training dataset using a structured geometric formal language, enabling broad coverage of geometric concepts and facilitating model adaptation. We explore two fine-tuning approaches: Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Our results show that GRPO significantly outperforms SFT by better aligning model behavior with task-specific rewards. Furthermore, we propose a verify-and-regenerate mechanism that detects incorrect predictions and re-infers answers using contextual reasoning history, further boosting accuracy. Notably, even state-of-the-art Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) struggle with this task, underscoring the necessity of explicitly evaluating and strengthening geometric grounding as a prerequisite for robust geometric problem solving.
