Moreover, models trained on GeoRef demonstrate measurable improvements on downstream geometric reasoning tasks, highlighting the broader value of REC as a foundation for multimodal mathematical understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "GeoRef: Referring Expressions in Geometry via Task Formulation, Synthetic Supervision, and Reinforced MLLM-based Solutions" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new task, Referring Expression Comprehension (REC), specifically designed to evaluate and improve the geometric grounding abilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). The authors present GeoRef, a benchmark dataset for this task, constructed using existing geometric problem corpora and supplemented with a large-scale synthetically generated training dataset using a formal geometric language. The paper explores fine-tuning strategies, including Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and a Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) based approach, demonstrating that GRPO significantly outperforms SFT. Furthermore, they introduce a verify-and-regenerate mechanism to refine incorrect predictions. The experiments show that existing MLLMs struggle with the REC task, but training on GeoRef improves performance not only on the REC task itself but also on downstream geometric reasoning tasks, suggesting REC can serve as valuable pretraining for multimodal mathematical understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel and well-motivated task – REC for geometric problems. Existing work on geometric problem solving often overlooks the crucial grounding step, directly jumping to reasoning without ensuring the model accurately understands the diagram. This task explicitly targets this neglected area. While REC itself is not a brand new concept in computer vision, its application to geometric diagrams with a focus on points, shapes, and spatial relationships is a significant contribution. The use of a geometric formal language for synthetic data generation is also a noteworthy approach, enabling the creation of a large, consistent training dataset where labeled data is typically scarce. However, the generation methodology relies on Penrose, which, while a powerful tool, is not fundamentally new in itself. The verify-and-regenerate mechanism, though promising, builds upon existing ideas of self-correction and verification in other domains.

*   **Significance:** The significance lies in addressing a fundamental bottleneck in geometric problem-solving: visual grounding. By explicitly evaluating and improving this ability, the paper potentially enables more robust and reliable geometric reasoning systems. The experiments convincingly show that performance on REC correlates with performance on downstream tasks, demonstrating the practical value of improving geometric grounding. The identification of weaknesses in existing MLLMs in the grounding task highlights the need for more research in this area. The GeoRef dataset and synthetic data generation pipeline provide valuable resources for future research. A limitation is that the improvement on downstream tasks, while present, is not revolutionary.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly defined and well-motivated task.
    *   High-quality benchmark dataset (GeoRef) with carefully designed annotations.
    *   Effective synthetic data generation approach using a formal geometric language.
    *   Demonstrated improvements on both REC and downstream geometric reasoning tasks.
    *   Thorough experimental evaluation with multiple models and ablation studies.
    *   Detailed analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The verify-and-regenerate mechanism, while helpful, isn't a radical departure from existing self-correction techniques.
    *   While the improvements on downstream tasks are statistically significant, they're not massive, suggesting that geometric grounding is only one piece of the puzzle. The overall accuracy on GeoRef test remains relatively low, even with fine-tuning.
    *   While the synthetic data generation uses Penrose, further innovation in automatically generating diverse *and* challenging (adversarial) REC examples could be explored.

*   **Impact and Influence:**
    *   The paper is likely to influence future research in geometric problem-solving by emphasizing the importance of geometric grounding.
    *   The GeoRef dataset will serve as a valuable benchmark for evaluating future models.
    *   The synthetic data generation approach could be adapted to other domains where labeled data is scarce.
    *   The findings will encourage further research into methods for improving the geometric grounding abilities of MLLMs.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the field by identifying and addressing a critical but often neglected aspect of geometric problem-solving. The novelty is reasonable, with the task definition and the application of synthetic data generation to this specific domain being valuable contributions. The significance is clear, with demonstrated improvements on downstream tasks. However, the improvements are not groundbreaking, and the individual components (GRPO, verify-and-regenerate) build upon existing techniques. While valuable resources were created, there exists opportunity for improvement in the generation of complex scenarios. Overall, the work presents a strong, well-executed study that will influence future research, but the incremental nature of some of the methodological components prevents it from achieving a higher score. It makes a definite step forward in geometric AI.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### When Instructions Multiply: Measuring and Estimating LLM Capabilities of Multiple Instructions Following
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21051v1)
- **Authors**: Keno Harada, Yudai Yamazaki, Masachika Taniguchi, Edison Marrese-Taylor, Takeshi Kojima, Yusuke Iwasawa, Yutaka Matsuo
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to real-world scenarios, it becomes crucial to understand their ability to follow multiple instructions simultaneously. To systematically evaluate these capabilities, we introduce two specialized benchmarks for fundamental domains where multiple instructions following is important: Many Instruction-Following Eval (ManyIFEval) for text generation with up to ten instructions, and Style-aware Mostly Basic Programming Problems (StyleMBPP) for code generation with up to six instructions. Our experiments with the created benchmarks across ten LLMs reveal that performance consistently degrades as the number of instructions increases. Furthermore, given the fact that evaluating all the possible combinations of multiple instructions is computationally impractical in actual use cases, we developed three types of regression models that can estimate performance on both unseen instruction combinations and different numbers of instructions which are not used during training. We demonstrate that a logistic regression model using instruction count as an explanatory variable can predict performance of following multiple instructions with approximately 10% error, even for unseen instruction combinations. We show that relatively modest sample sizes (500 for ManyIFEval and 300 for StyleMBPP) are sufficient for performance estimation, enabling efficient evaluation of LLMs under various instruction combinations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces two new benchmarks, ManyIFEval and StyleMBPP, designed to evaluate the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs) to simultaneously follow multiple instructions in text generation and code generation tasks, respectively. ManyIFEval, extending IFEval, assesses text generation with up to 10 instructions, while StyleMBPP, derived from MBPP, evaluates code generation with up to 6 instructions emphasizing adherence to coding style guidelines. The authors conduct experiments across ten LLMs, revealing that performance consistently degrades as the number of instructions increases. Furthermore, they develop regression models to estimate performance on unseen instruction combinations, demonstrating that a logistic regression model using instruction count as an explanatory variable can predict performance with reasonable accuracy even for unseen combinations, and that relatively small sample sizes are sufficient for performance estimation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Controlled Experimental Design:** The paper's main strength is its controlled experimental design. By keeping the core task description consistent while varying the number of instructions, the authors successfully isolate the impact of instruction count on model performance.
    *   **Objective Evaluation:** The benchmarks employ programmatic rule-based verification, ensuring objective and reliable assessment of model performance, addressing a key limitation in previous works that relied on LLM-as-a-judge methods.
    *   **Performance Estimation:** The development of regression models to estimate performance on unseen instruction combinations is a valuable contribution, addressing the computational impracticality of evaluating all possible instruction combinations in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Valuable Datasets:**  The creation of the two benchmarks, and making them publically available, is a valuable resource for the LLM research community.
    *   **Clear Problem Formulation:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of evaluating multiple-instruction-following capabilities and the need for better evaluation methods.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Instruction Simplicity:** The instructions used in the benchmarks are relatively simple and focused on surface-level aspects like formatting and keyword inclusion. The paper acknowledges the need for more complex instruction types, involving semantic understanding and conditional logic. While justifiable for controlled experimentation, the simplicity limits the real-world applicability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Mechanistic Explanation:** While the paper demonstrates the performance degradation with increasing instruction count, it offers limited mechanistic explanations for *why* this occurs. The authors suggest directions for future work, such as analyzing attention patterns, but the current analysis remains largely empirical.
    *   **Incremental Novelty in Benchmark Development:** The benchmarks build upon existing datasets (IFEval and MBPP). While the modifications are significant in terms of controlled experimental design, the core tasks remain the same. The novelty lies more in the experimental methodology than in the creation of entirely new, complex tasks.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Reasoning for StyleMBPP:** The paper mentioned that reasoning models performed better on style instruction following. It would be valuable to analyze the generated code and reasoning traces to demonstrate if and how style instructions are being followed.

*   **Significance:**
    *   The paper contributes to the growing body of work evaluating LLM capabilities beyond single-task performance. The focus on multiple-instruction following is crucial for practical applications.
    *   The controlled experimental design and objective evaluation methodology provide a valuable framework for future benchmark development in this area.
    *   The performance estimation techniques offer a practical approach to evaluating LLMs under various instruction combinations, reducing computational costs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the evaluation of LLMs, particularly by emphasizing the importance of evaluating multiple-instruction following capability in a controlled manner and by offering relatively low cost performance estimation tools. The carefully crafted benchmarks and the analysis of factors influencing performance, as well as the public release of the benchmarks, provide a strong foundation for future research. The main weaknesses are the instruction set simplicity and a limited mechanistic insight. Given the value and importance of the findings, a score of 7 is appropriate.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Disagreements in Reasoning: How a Model's Thinking Process Dictates Persuasion in Multi-Agent Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21054v1)
- **Authors**: Haodong Zhao, Jidong Li, Zhaomin Wu, Tianjie Ju, Zhuosheng Zhang, Bingsheng He, Gongshen Liu
- **Abstract**: The rapid proliferation of recent Multi-Agent Systems (MAS), where Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) usually collaborate to solve complex problems, necessitates a deep understanding of the persuasion dynamics that govern their interactions. This paper challenges the prevailing hypothesis that persuasive efficacy is primarily a function of model scale. We propose instead that these dynamics are fundamentally dictated by a model's underlying cognitive process, especially its capacity for explicit reasoning. Through a series of multi-agent persuasion experiments, we uncover a fundamental trade-off we term the Persuasion Duality. Our findings reveal that the reasoning process in LRMs exhibits significantly greater resistance to persuasion, maintaining their initial beliefs more robustly. Conversely, making this reasoning process transparent by sharing the "thinking content" dramatically increases their ability to persuade others. We further consider more complex transmission persuasion situations and reveal complex dynamics of influence propagation and decay within multi-hop persuasion between multiple agent networks. This research provides systematic evidence linking a model's internal processing architecture to its external persuasive behavior, offering a novel explanation for the susceptibility of advanced models and highlighting critical implications for the safety, robustness, and design of future MAS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Disagreements in Reasoning: How a Model's Thinking Process Dictates Persuasion in Multi-Agent Systems":

**Summary:**

This paper challenges the prevalent notion that persuasive efficacy in Large Language Models (LLMs) and Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) is primarily driven by model scale. Instead, the authors argue that a model's underlying cognitive process, particularly its capacity for explicit reasoning, fundamentally dictates persuasion dynamics in Multi-Agent Systems (MAS). Through experiments, they reveal a "Persuasion Duality," where LRMs, while more resistant to persuasion due to their robust reasoning, can also be more persuasive if their "thinking content" is shared. The paper further investigates the complexities of persuasion in multi-agent networks, exploring influence propagation and decay. The research systematically links a model's internal processing to its persuasive behavior, offering an explanation for advanced model susceptibility and highlighting implications for the design, safety, and robustness of future MAS. It also proposes and validates a prompt-based mitigation technique to enhance resistance to persuasion by promoting logical rigor.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's central argument – that a model's reasoning process, not just scale, is crucial for persuasion – is a significant departure from existing assumptions. The "Persuasion Duality" concept is a novel and insightful framework for understanding the trade-offs in agent design. The study of persuasion dynamics in multi-agent *networks*, going beyond simple pairwise interactions, is also a notable contribution. Furthermore, the mechanism for enhancing resistance based on adversarially detecting arguments is a practical, and potentially broadly applicable, contribution.
*   **Significance:** Understanding persuasion dynamics is increasingly critical as LLMs become more integrated into autonomous systems. The paper addresses an important concern, that larger model scales do not guarantee improved robustness against undesirable persuasion, and provides insights to improve the reliability and safety of MAS. The exploration of persuasion in multi-agent *networks* anticipates the increasing complexity of future applications and is important. The prompt-based mitigation technique offers a practical approach to enhancing LRM robustness and suggests valuable directions for future research. The linking of internal cognitive architecture to external behaviors is significant.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined research questions:** The paper clearly articulates its objectives through the two guiding research questions.
    *   **Rigorous methodology:** The authors conduct a thorough empirical study with a diverse set of models and datasets, enhancing the generalizability of their findings.
    *   **Compelling evidence:** The experimental results provide strong support for the "Persuasion Duality" and other claims.
    *   **Practical implications:** The paper offers valuable insights for designing safer and more resilient MAS and a usable prompt based method for improving the robustness of the underlying models.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited to specific tasks:** The experiments are conducted on a specific set of objective and subjective tasks. While the results are promising, further investigation across a broader range of tasks and domains would be beneficial.
    *   **Prompt dependence:** The prompt-based mitigation technique is effective but may be sensitive to the specific wording and design of the prompt.
    *   **Limited analysis of model internal states:** Although the paper discusses the link between internal reasoning and persuasion, a deeper analysis of the internal representations and processing within the models could provide more nuanced insights.
    *   **Simplicity of multi-agent chains:** The multi-agent chains explored are relatively simple and may not capture the full complexity of real-world MAS interactions.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence research on LLMs and MAS by shifting the focus from model scale to cognitive architecture and reasoning processes. The "Persuasion Duality" framework could become a key concept in agent design, and the prompt-based mitigation technique could inspire further research on enhancing LRM robustness. It opens up new directions for studying influence and manipulation in complex AI systems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field by challenging existing assumptions about persuasion in LLMs and introducing a new framework for understanding its dynamics. The empirical evidence is compelling, and the practical implications are valuable. However, the paper's limitations, such as its focus on specific tasks, the prompt dependence of its mitigation technique, the limited depth of the internal analysis, and the simplicity of its agent network analysis, prevent it from receiving a higher score. Nevertheless, the paper's strengths outweigh its weaknesses, making it a valuable and influential contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### PMark: Towards Robust and Distortion-free Semantic-level Watermarking with Channel Constraints
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21057v1)
- **Authors**: Jiahao Huo, Shuliang Liu, Bin Wang, Junyan Zhang, Yibo Yan, Aiwei Liu, Xuming Hu, Mingxun Zhou
- **Abstract**: Semantic-level watermarking (SWM) for large language models (LLMs) enhances watermarking robustness against text modifications and paraphrasing attacks by treating the sentence as the fundamental unit. However, existing methods still lack strong theoretical guarantees of robustness, and reject-sampling-based generation often introduces significant distribution distortions compared with unwatermarked outputs. In this work, we introduce a new theoretical framework on SWM through the concept of proxy functions (PFs) $\unicode{x2013}$ functions that map sentences to scalar values. Building on this framework, we propose PMark, a simple yet powerful SWM method that estimates the PF median for the next sentence dynamically through sampling while enforcing multiple PF constraints (which we call channels) to strengthen watermark evidence. Equipped with solid theoretical guarantees, PMark achieves the desired distortion-free property and improves the robustness against paraphrasing-style attacks. We also provide an empirically optimized version that further removes the requirement for dynamical median estimation for better sampling efficiency. Experimental results show that PMark consistently outperforms existing SWM baselines in both text quality and robustness, offering a more effective paradigm for detecting machine-generated text. Our code will be released at [this URL](https://github.com/PMark-repo/PMark).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "PMARK: TOWARDS ROBUST AND DISTORTION-FREE SEMANTIC-LEVEL WATERMARKING WITH CHANNEL CONSTRAINTS":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PMARK, a new semantic-level watermarking (SWM) method for large language models (LLMs).  It establishes a theoretical framework for analyzing SWM schemes using proxy functions (PFs). PMARK defines the PF of a sentence as the cosine similarity between its embedding and a random vector. The method dynamically estimates the PF median for the next sentence by sampling and enforcing multiple PF constraints (channels) to strengthen watermark evidence. It offers theoretical guarantees of distortion-free generation and improved robustness against paraphrasing attacks. The paper includes both an online (dynamic median estimation) and an offline (prior median) version, with the latter reducing computational costs.  Experimental results demonstrate PMARK's superior performance compared to existing SWM baselines regarding text quality and robustness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates good novelty across several aspects.  The framework using proxy functions offers a novel lens through which to analyze and potentially unify existing SWM methods. The insight that sparse watermarking evidence negatively impacts robustness and the subsequent introduction of multi-channel constraints to address this are significant contributions. The development of both online and offline versions is a practical consideration, but less significant in terms of core novelty. The use of cosine similarity and median estimation itself is not particularly novel, but its combination within this specific framework and task is.

* **Significance:**  The problem addressed – robust and distortion-free watermarking – is highly relevant given concerns about AI-generated content traceability and copyright. PMARK addresses key weaknesses in current SWM techniques (vulnerability to paraphrasing and distortion) which makes this advancement highly significant.  The theoretical guarantees regarding distortion-free generation, even with simplified median estimation, are important for adoption, as they offer a more principled approach than heuristics. The empirical results, showcasing PMARK's improved robustness without sacrificing text quality, strengthens its potential impact. The reduction in computational cost through the offline version helps bridge the gap between research and real-world deployment.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Solid theoretical foundation:** The paper offers theoretical grounding through proxy functions and distortion-free properties.
    * **Practical considerations:** The development of online and offline versions demonstrates awareness of real-world deployment constraints.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** Extensive experiments across multiple datasets, backbones, and attack types provide strong evidence for PMARK's effectiveness.
    * **Clear problem framing:** The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing SWM methods and articulates how PMARK addresses those limitations.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Prior Median Assumption:** While the paper presents a concentration of measure argument for the prior median, it acknowledges a lack of strict theoretical error bounds for this assumption. The empirical validation helps mitigate this, but a more rigorous theoretical justification would be preferred. This presents a potential limitation on its use, if the median estimation is not valid the text becomes detectable.
    * **Reliance on Sentence Segmentation:** PMARK depends on accurate sentence segmentation during both generation and detection. Performance would suffer if sentence boundary detection is poor or inconsistent. More discussion on how to handle imperfect sentence segmentation is needed.

* **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to significantly influence research and development in AI watermarking. The theoretical framework can guide future algorithm design and analysis. The practical improvements in robustness and efficiency may lead to broader adoption of SWM techniques for AI-generated content.

* **Score Rationale:**

PMARK demonstrates a high degree of both novelty and practical significance, it is a meaningful step forward in the field of semantic-level watermarking. While there are limitations related to the prior median assumption and sentence segmentation dependency, the paper's strengths outweigh these weaknesses.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### Designing for Novice Debuggers: A Pilot Study on an AI-Assisted Debugging Tool
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21067v1)
- **Authors**: Oka Kurniawan, Erick Chandra, Christopher M. Poskitt, Yannic Noller, Kenny Tsu Wei Choo, Cyrille Jegourel
- **Abstract**: Debugging is a fundamental skill that novice programmers must develop. Numerous tools have been created to assist novice programmers in this process. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have been integrated with automated program repair techniques to generate fixes for students' buggy code. However, many of these tools foster an over-reliance on AI and do not actively engage students in the debugging process. In this work, we aim to design an intuitive debugging assistant, CodeHinter, that combines traditional debugging tools with LLM-based techniques to help novice debuggers fix semantic errors while promoting active engagement in the debugging process. We present findings from our second design iteration, which we tested with a group of undergraduate students. Our results indicate that the students found the tool highly effective in resolving semantic errors and significantly easier to use than the first version. Consistent with our previous study, error localization was the most valuable feature. Finally, we conclude that any AI-assisted debugging tool should be personalized based on user profiles to optimize their interactions with students.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents CODEHINTER, an AI-assisted debugging tool designed to help novice programmers fix semantic errors while actively engaging them in the debugging process. CODEHINTER combines traditional debugging tools with LLM-based techniques, offering features like an 'End-to-End Test' button, LLM-powered hints and quizzes, line highlighting with code difference display, spectrum-based fault localization, and LLM-generated pseudo-code. A pilot study involving undergraduate students showed that CODEHINTER was effective in resolving semantic errors and easier to use than a previous version. The study highlighted the value of error localization and suggested that AI-assisted debugging tools should be personalized based on user profiles.  The goal is to avoid over-reliance on AI solutions by guiding students through the debugging process.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon existing work in AI-assisted debugging and automated program repair.  The novelty lies primarily in the *integration* of several techniques into a unified tool and the emphasis on *active learning* rather than automated fixing. The features, such as the 'End-to-End Test', are a good attempt to integrate testing into the workflow.  The combination of fault localization, LLM-based hints, and quizzes is also a somewhat novel combination.  However, the individual components (LLMs, fault localization, etc.) are not groundbreaking.  The use of print statements over a debugger is also not new but the LLM help to create the statement has merit.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a significant problem: novice programmers often struggle with debugging, especially semantic errors.  By focusing on *active engagement* and *guided learning*, the paper aims to improve debugging skills rather than simply providing solutions. The study suggests that such an approach can be beneficial.  The findings regarding error localization's importance are consistent with previous research and reinforce its value. The call for personalized AI-assisted debugging tools is also a relevant direction. However, the scale of the user study is a limitation. The tool is implemented with a specific LLM model, meaning performance depends on the capabilities of that model. It is unknown how it would perform with different models. There is limited discussion on scaling for different problems of varying complexity.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper addresses an important problem in programming education.
    *   The design of CODEHINTER is well-motivated and incorporates several useful features.
    *   The user study provides evidence that the tool is effective and user-friendly.
    *   The emphasis on active learning and personalized assistance is valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The sample size of the user study is small (10 participants), limiting the generalizability of the results.
    *   The reliance on a specific LLM (GPT-40) means the tool's performance may vary depending on the LLM's capabilities and any changes to its behavior over time.
    *   The paper could provide a more in-depth analysis of the impact of CODEHINTER on learning outcomes (e.g., improved debugging skills, reduced reliance on AI).
    *   The paper could include a broader discussion of the ethical considerations of using AI in debugging, such as the potential for bias or the impact on students' self-efficacy.
    *   The paper does not provide any discussion on how the tool would scale as students progress and face more complex problems.
    *   The limitations on the LLM (OpenAI's GPT-40) performance are not known.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the design of future AI-assisted debugging tools by highlighting the importance of active learning and personalized assistance. The findings regarding error localization could also inform the development of more effective debugging strategies for novice programmers. The study can also encourage further research on how to effectively integrate AI into programming education.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a valuable contribution by demonstrating how AI can be used to support novice programmers in debugging while promoting active learning.  The design of CODEHINTER is innovative, and the user study provides encouraging results. However, the small sample size and limited evaluation of learning outcomes limit the paper's overall impact. The paper could have significantly strengthened its contribution with a larger scale user study that measures gains in debug skill. The novelty is incremental, given existing research in this space.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### Normalizing Flows are Capable Visuomotor Policy Learning Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21073v1)
- **Authors**: Simon Kristoffersson Lind, Jialong Li, Maj Stenmark, Volker Krüger
- **Abstract**: The field of general purpose robotics has recently embraced powerful probabilistic models, such as diffusion models, to model and learn complex behaviors. However, these models often come with significant trade-offs, namely high computational costs for inference and a fundamental inability to quantify output uncertainty. We argue that a model's trustworthiness, a critical factor for reliable, general-purpose robotics, is inherently linked to its ability to provide confidence measures. In this work, we introduce Normalizing Flows Policy, a novel visuomotor policy learning model based on Normalizing Flows. We show that Normalizing Flows are a natural and powerful alternative to diffusion models, providing both a statistically sound measure of confidence and a highly efficient inference process. Through comprehensive experiments across four distinct simulated robotic tasks, we demonstrate that Normalizing Flows Policy achieves performance comparable to, and often surpassing, Diffusion Policy, and it does so not only with improved sample efficiency but also with up to 30 times faster inference. Additionally, our ablation study validates several key architectural and training techniques that enable Normalizing Flows to perform well in this domain.
- **Summary**: Okay, let's break down this paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Normalizing Flows Policy (NF-P), a novel visuomotor policy learning model leveraging Normalizing Flows (NFs) as an alternative to the more prevalent Diffusion Policy (DP). The authors argue that NFs offer key advantages over diffusion models, particularly in providing statistically sound confidence measures and enabling significantly faster inference. They demonstrate through experiments across four simulated robotic tasks using RoboTwin 2.0 that NF-P achieves performance comparable to, and sometimes surpassing, DP, especially when training data is limited. The paper also validates several architectural and training techniques crucial for achieving strong performance with NFs in visuomotor policy learning, and includes an ablation study to analyze the impact of various design choices. The results suggest that NF-P is a sample-efficient, fast-inference alternative to diffusion models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty:**  While Normalizing Flows are not entirely new to robotics, their application to *visuomotor policy learning*, especially as a direct competitor to diffusion models, is a relatively unexplored area. The design choices for NF-P and the exploration of techniques for performance and stability make the work relevant.
    *   **Significance:** Diffusion models, despite their performance, suffer from limitations in inference speed and the lack of uncertainty measures, both crucial for real-world robotic applications. NF-P tackles these issues directly by leveraging the inherent properties of NFs. The demonstration of comparable or better performance with faster inference times and built-in confidence measures is a significant step forward.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The use of a well-established simulation environment (RoboTwin 2.0) and the variety of robotic tasks provide a good basis for evaluating the performance of NF-P. The comparison with Diffusion Policy and the ablation study further strengthen the experimental results.
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:**  The authors explicitly state the limitations of diffusion models and position NFs as a solution with clear advantages. This enhances the clarity and focus of the paper.
    *  **Architectural Details:** The details regarding the model architecture choices, training procedures, the use of CNNs and neural spline flows enhance the reproducibility of the results.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Real-World Validation:**  The experiments are conducted exclusively in simulation. While RoboTwin 2.0 provides a valuable platform, the ultimate test is real-world performance. The sim-to-real gap remains a significant challenge. While the paper tackles the inherent issues of DP like inference time and lack of confidence measure, real-world data can be noisy, contain different image textures or lighting which simulation would not be able to capture.
    *   **Incremental Improvements:**  The architectural changes are somewhat incremental. While the authors validate certain design choices, the novelty primarily stems from the application area rather than revolutionary architectural innovations. Techniques such as Noise addition or using newer, powerful, architectures such as transformers can further improve the performance.
    *   **Limited Architecture Evaluation:** The authors use only one type of normalizing flow (Neural Spline Flows) and a single base architecture. The performance of other types of normalizing flows, coupled with different architectures such as transformers could have given more detailed insights.

**Justification of Score:**

Considering both strengths and weaknesses, I would assign a score of **7**. The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating a viable and potentially superior alternative to diffusion models for visuomotor policy learning. The claims are well-supported by the experimental results. However, the lack of real-world validation and the somewhat incremental nature of the architectural innovations prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the work provides strong evidence for the potential of NFs in this domain, further research is needed to address the sim-to-real gap and explore the full potential of NFs.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.RO
- **Score**: 7/10

### RePro: Leveraging Large Language Models for Semi-Automated Reproduction of Networking Research Results
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21074v1)
- **Authors**: Yining Jiang, Wenyun Xu, Qingyu Song, Yuling Lin, Xuanhao Liu, Xiaoqiang Zheng, Qiang Su, Lizhao You, Lu Tang, Wangjian Feng, Linghe Kong, Qiao Xiang, Jiwu Shu
- **Abstract**: Reproducing networking research is a critical but challenging task due to the scarcity of open-source code. While Large Language Models (LLMs) can automate code generation, current approaches lack the generalizability required for the diverse networking field. To address this, we propose RePro, a semi-automated reproduction framework that leverages advanced prompt engineering to reproduce network systems from their research papers. RePro combines few-shot in-context learning with Structured and Semantic Chain of Thought (SCoT/SeCoT) techniques to systematically translate a paper's description into an optimized, executable implementation. The framework operates through a three-stage pipeline: system description extraction, structural code generation, and code optimization. Our evaluation with five state-of-the-art LLMs across diverse network sub-domains demonstrates that RePro significantly reduces reproduction time compared to manual efforts while achieving comparable system performance, validating its effectiveness and efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary**

The paper introduces RePro, a semi-automated framework leveraging large language models (LLMs) to reproduce networking research results. The core motivation stems from the difficulty in reproducing networking papers due to limited open-source code availability.  RePro aims to bridge this gap by using advanced prompt engineering to translate paper descriptions into executable code. It operates in a three-stage pipeline: system description extraction, structural code generation (using Structured and Semantic Chain-of-Thought techniques), and code optimization. The authors evaluate RePro using five LLMs across diverse network sub-domains and demonstrate a reduction in reproduction time compared to manual efforts while achieving comparable system performance.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel framework (RePro) tailored to the networking domain, which addresses a significant challenge: the reproducibility crisis due to limited open-source code. While other works explore LLMs for code generation, RePro distinguishes itself by incorporating domain-specific knowledge and architectural considerations inherent to networking systems. The systematic approach to prompt engineering, combining few-shot learning with Structured and Semantic Chain-of-Thought, is a key contribution. However, it builds upon established prompt engineering techniques, and the novelty lies primarily in their application and adaptation to this particular problem.

*   **Significance:** Improving reproducibility is crucial for advancing scientific progress. RePro has the potential to significantly reduce the time and effort required to validate and build upon existing networking research. By automating code generation, it lowers the barrier to entry for researchers and practitioners, fostering collaboration and innovation. The paper offers concrete evidence of RePro's effectiveness, demonstrating significant time savings and comparable system performance. However, the impact on the community will depend on the tool's adoption and sustained development.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a real-world problem:** The paper tackles the important issue of reproducibility in networking research.
    *   **Well-defined framework:** RePro provides a systematic approach to code generation from research papers.
    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The framework is evaluated across multiple LLMs and diverse network sub-domains.
    *   **Demonstrated effectiveness:** The results show significant time savings and comparable system performance.
    *   **Structured approach to prompt engineering:**  The integration of few-shot learning, SCoT and SeCoT are well-defined and contribute to the framework's capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Semi-automated nature:**  RePro still requires human intervention, particularly for error correction and refinement.  While the paper quantifies this effort, more details about the types of errors and the expertise required for correction would be valuable.
    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** The framework's performance is inherently limited by the capabilities of the underlying LLMs.  The results may vary depending on the specific LLM used and its training data.
    *   **Limited scope:** The evaluation is limited to specific research papers.  The generalizability of RePro to a broader range of networking systems needs further investigation.
    *   **Adoption concerns:**  The success of RePro hinges on its adoption by the research community.  Factors such as ease of use, documentation, and community support will be crucial for widespread adoption.
    *   **Evaluation metrics:** While system performance is evaluated, more detail on specific cases of failure to reproduce results would be useful to understand edge cases where the tool performs poorly.

*   **Potential Influence:** RePro has the potential to become a valuable tool for the networking research community, facilitating reproducibility and accelerating innovation. Its influence will depend on its adoption and further development. If successful, it could serve as a model for similar frameworks in other scientific domains.

**Score:** 7

**Rationale:**

RePro addresses a significant problem in networking research with a novel and well-defined framework. The experimental results demonstrate its effectiveness in reducing reproduction time and achieving comparable system performance. The framework's structured approach to prompt engineering, combining various techniques, shows a clear understanding of the problem and LLM capabilities. However, the semi-automated nature and reliance on LLMs, along with limitations in the evaluation scope and adoption concerns, prevent it from receiving a higher score. The novelty, while present, is incremental rather than groundbreaking.

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field, but its ultimate significance will depend on further refinement, broader adoption, and its adaptability to more diverse and complex networking systems.

- **Classification**: cs.NI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Communication Bias in Large Language Models: A Regulatory Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21075v1)
- **Authors**: Adrian Kuenzler, Stefan Schmid
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly central to many applications, raising concerns about bias, fairness, and regulatory compliance. This paper reviews risks of biased outputs and their societal impact, focusing on frameworks like the EU's AI Act and the Digital Services Act. We argue that beyond constant regulation, stronger attention to competition and design governance is needed to ensure fair, trustworthy AI. This is a preprint of the Communications of the ACM article of the same title.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Communication Bias in Large Language Models: A Regulatory Perspective" examines the risks associated with biased outputs from Large Language Models (LLMs) and their implications for society. It analyzes how emerging regulatory frameworks, such as the EU AI Act and the Digital Services Act (DSA), aim to address these challenges through transparency, accountability, and harm mitigation requirements. It argues that LLMs, serving as primary interfaces between users and AI, can subtly influence opinions, potentially leading to echo chambers and societal polarization. The authors emphasize that current regulations often treat communication bias as a byproduct of broader safety and content moderation measures and advocate for a more comprehensive approach that includes value chain regulation, content moderation, competition, and technology design governance to foster fair and trustworthy AI systems. The paper also explores the limitations of existing legal frameworks and proposes measures to promote diversity and representativeness in LLM design.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper addresses an important and timely issue, the potential for LLMs to generate communication bias and its broader societal ramifications. However, the individual elements it discusses – the risks of LLMs, the existing regulatory landscape in the EU, and the suggested mitigation strategies – are not entirely novel in isolation. The novelty lies primarily in **synthesizing these aspects** to highlight the specific challenge of communication bias and arguing for a more holistic regulatory approach. Other works have addressed bias in AI/ML, and some discuss LLMs in particular. However, **the paper is unique in bringing together these elements, focusing on the unique risks communication bias poses.** The paper does not present novel technological solutions, but rather a policy and regulatory perspective on an existing problem.

**Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its potential to influence policy and regulatory discussions around AI and LLMs. By explicitly identifying and defining "communication bias," it provides a clearer framework for policymakers to address this specific type of harm. The analysis of the EU AI Act and DSA, highlighting their strengths and limitations, is valuable for understanding the current regulatory landscape. The call for a more comprehensive approach that goes beyond traditional content moderation and incorporates competition policy and technology design governance is a significant contribution to the discussion. Also, by outlining that regulation should be aimed at not only mitigating the risks associated with AI but also promoting the benefits can serve to influence policymakers and industry leaders.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear and Accessible:** The paper is well-written and accessible to a broad audience, including policymakers, legal scholars, and AI researchers.
*   **Comprehensive Overview:** It provides a good overview of the risks associated with communication bias and the relevant regulatory frameworks.
*   **Policy-Oriented:** The paper focuses on practical policy recommendations and offers concrete suggestions for improving the regulatory landscape.
*   **Timely:** Given the rapid development of LLMs and the increasing attention to AI regulation, the paper addresses a pressing issue.
*   **Well-researched**: The paper references a variety of documents and articles to demonstrate the existing state of affairs and to ground their arguments.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Empirical Evidence:** The paper relies primarily on theoretical arguments and does not present extensive empirical evidence to support its claims about the prevalence or impact of communication bias.
*   **Lack of Technological Depth:** While discussing the risks of LLMs, the paper doesn't delve deeply into the technical aspects of how bias arises in these models or how they could be mitigated through technical solutions.
*   **EU-Centric Focus:** While justified by the EU's leading role in AI regulation, the strong focus on the EU context may limit the paper's generalizability to other jurisdictions.
*   **Overly Broad Recommendations:** The call for a more comprehensive approach is somewhat vague and lacks specific details on how to implement the proposed changes. It would be further improved with concrete examples and case studies.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the discussion surrounding AI regulation by focusing on the specific issue of communication bias in LLMs and suggesting a more holistic regulatory approach. However, the lack of empirical evidence, the limited technological depth, and the somewhat broad recommendations limit its overall impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### SoM-1K: A Thousand-Problem Benchmark Dataset for Strength of Materials
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21079v1)
- **Authors**: Qixin Wan, Zilong Wang, Jingwen Zhou, Wanting Wang, Ziheng Geng, Jiachen Liu, Ran Cao, Minghui Cheng, Lu Cheng
- **Abstract**: Foundation models have shown remarkable capabilities in various domains, but their performance on complex, multimodal engineering problems remains largely unexplored. We introduce SoM-1K, the first large-scale multimodal benchmark dataset dedicated to evaluating foundation models on problems in the strength of materials (SoM). The dataset, which contains 1,065 annotated SoM problems, mirrors real-world engineering tasks by including both textual problem statements and schematic diagrams. Due to the limited capabilities of current foundation models in understanding complicated visual information, we propose a novel prompting strategy called Descriptions of Images (DoI), which provides rigorous expert-generated text descriptions of the visual diagrams as the context. We evaluate eight representative foundation models, including both large language models (LLMs) and vision language models (VLMs). Our results show that current foundation models struggle significantly with these engineering problems, with the best-performing model achieving only 56.6% accuracy. Interestingly, we found that LLMs, when provided with DoI, often outperform VLMs provided with visual diagrams. A detailed error analysis reveals that DoI plays a crucial role in mitigating visual misinterpretation errors, suggesting that accurate text-based descriptions can be more effective than direct image input for current foundation models. This work establishes a rigorous benchmark for engineering AI and highlights a critical need for developing more robust multimodal reasoning capabilities in foundation models, particularly in scientific and engineering contexts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SoM-1K, a new large-scale multimodal benchmark dataset for evaluating foundation models on strength of materials (SoM) problems. The dataset includes text, equations, and engineering diagrams, mirroring real-world engineering tasks.  The authors evaluate several LLMs and VLMs on the dataset and find that current models struggle with these problems. They propose a novel prompting strategy, Descriptions of Images (DoI), which provides expert-generated text descriptions of the visual diagrams. They find that LLMs, when provided with DoI, can outperform VLMs provided with visual diagrams directly, suggesting that current foundation models struggle with direct image interpretation in this domain. The paper concludes by highlighting the need for more robust multimodal reasoning capabilities in foundation models, especially for scientific and engineering contexts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's core novelty lies in creating a specialized, large-scale multimodal dataset (SoM-1K) tailored to engineering problems, specifically in the domain of strength of materials. Existing benchmarks often focus on general knowledge or natural images, lacking the specific visual and textual complexities of engineering schematics. The DoI prompting strategy is a practical contribution to improve model performance given limitations in handling visual information directly.

* **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from addressing a critical gap in evaluating and improving AI systems for engineering applications. Highlighting the shortcomings of existing foundation models in this domain is important because reliability and accuracy are paramount in engineering where errors can have serious consequences. The dataset provides a platform for future research to develop more robust and reliable AI tools for engineers. The finding that LLMs with textual descriptions can outperform VLMs points to a specific weakness of VLMs and provides a direction for improvement. It identifies a disconnect between general vision understanding and domain-specific schematic understanding.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Dataset Creation:** The construction of SoM-1K is a major strength, providing a valuable resource for the community. The careful selection of problems from textbooks and competitions ensures both breadth and complexity. The annotation process, including the DoI, adds significant value.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates a diverse range of foundation models and prompting strategies.
    * **Insightful Analysis:** The error analysis provides valuable insights into the types of errors that models make, which can inform future research directions (e.g., improving knowledge retrieval, arithmetic calculation, and extraction of information).

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Engineering Domains:**  While SoM is a critical field, the benchmark is limited to a single domain within engineering. Expanding the scope to other areas (e.g., electrical engineering, thermodynamics) would further enhance the dataset's impact.
    * **Model Selection:** The model selection is reasonable but could be expanded to include more recent and specialized VLMs and LLMs if available.
    * **DoI Dependence:** While DoI improves performance, it relies on expert-generated text, which is not scalable for real-world applications. Developing methods that automatically extract accurate descriptions from images would be more impactful.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    * **Driving Research:** SoM-1K serves as a benchmark for future research in multimodal engineering AI.
    * **Guiding Model Development:** The insights gained from the error analysis can inform the development of more robust and accurate foundation models for engineering applications.
    * **Raising Awareness:** The paper highlights the limitations of current models in a critical domain, raising awareness among AI researchers and engineers alike.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of engineering AI through the introduction of the SoM-1K dataset and the demonstration of the DoI prompting strategy. The dataset addresses a gap in existing benchmarks and provides a valuable resource for evaluating and improving foundation models. The findings that LLMs with textual descriptions can outperform VLMs is insightful. However, the reliance on expert-generated DoI limits the scalability of the approach, and the scope of the benchmark is confined to a single engineering domain. This limits the score to a 7, as while impactful, there are clear avenues for improvement and broadening the scope. The dataset itself is a significant contribution, but the specific solution still needs more development to be generally useful.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Which Cultural Lens Do Models Adopt? On Cultural Positioning Bias and Agentic Mitigation in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21080v1)
- **Authors**: Yixin Wan, Xingrun Chen, Kai-Wei Chang
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have unlocked a wide range of downstream generative applications. However, we found that they also risk perpetuating subtle fairness issues tied to culture, positioning their generations from the perspectives of the mainstream US culture while demonstrating salient externality towards non-mainstream ones. In this work, we identify and systematically investigate this novel culture positioning bias, in which an LLM's default generative stance aligns with a mainstream view and treats other cultures as outsiders. We propose the CultureLens benchmark with 4000 generation prompts and 3 evaluation metrics for quantifying this bias through the lens of a culturally situated interview script generation task, in which an LLM is positioned as an onsite reporter interviewing local people across 10 diverse cultures. Empirical evaluation on 5 state-of-the-art LLMs reveals a stark pattern: while models adopt insider tones in over 88 percent of US-contexted scripts on average, they disproportionately adopt mainly outsider stances for less dominant cultures. To resolve these biases, we propose 2 inference-time mitigation methods: a baseline prompt-based Fairness Intervention Pillars (FIP) method, and a structured Mitigation via Fairness Agents (MFA) framework consisting of 2 pipelines: (1) MFA-SA (Single-Agent) introduces a self-reflection and rewriting loop based on fairness guidelines. (2) MFA-MA (Multi-Agent) structures the process into a hierarchy of specialized agents: a Planner Agent(initial script generation), a Critique Agent (evaluates initial script against fairness pillars), and a Refinement Agent (incorporates feedback to produce a polished, unbiased script). Empirical results showcase the effectiveness of agent-based methods as a promising direction for mitigating biases in generative LLMs.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces the concept of "cultural positioning bias" in large language models (LLMs), where models disproportionately adopt the perspectives of dominant cultures (specifically, US culture) while treating other cultures as outsiders in generative tasks. The authors systematically investigate this bias using a novel benchmark called CULTURELENS, which comprises 4,000 generation prompts spanning 10 diverse cultures. The benchmark involves LLMs generating interview scripts, which are then evaluated to determine whether the LLM adopts an "insider" or "outsider" perspective. They propose three metrics: Cultural Externality Percentage (CEP), Cultural Perspective Deviation (CPD), and Cultural Alignment Gap (CAG) to quantify the bias. Empirical results across five state-of-the-art LLMs reveal a consistent pattern: models predominantly adopt insider perspectives in US contexts but default to outsider positioning for other cultures. To mitigate this bias, the authors propose two inference-time methods: a prompt-based Fairness Intervention Pillars (FIP) method and a Mitigation via Fairness Agents (MFA) framework. The MFA framework has two pipelines: MFA-SA (Single-Agent) and MFA-MA (Multi-Agent), which involve self-reflection, rewriting loops, and hierarchical agents to refine generated scripts. Experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of agent-based methods, particularly MFA, in reducing cultural positioning bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its identification and systematic investigation of a previously underexplored bias in LLMs: cultural positioning bias. While existing work has focused on Western-centric values and explicit cultural stereotypes, the concept of models implicitly adopting a viewpoint ("insider" vs. "outsider") is relatively novel. The CULTURELENS benchmark provides a concrete means of quantifying this bias and demonstrates its prevalence. The agent-based mitigation approach is also a valuable contribution, building upon existing prompt engineering and knowledge augmentation methods.

**Significance:** The findings have significant implications for the responsible development and deployment of LLMs, especially as they are increasingly used in diverse cultural contexts. Perpetuating subtle biases like cultural positioning could exacerbate existing inequalities and reinforce cultural hegemony. The mitigation strategies proposed offer promising avenues for addressing these issues. The paper raises awareness about the importance of considering the implicit cultural lens of LLMs, which is crucial for fostering more equitable and inclusive AI systems.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clearly Defined Problem:** The paper clearly defines and articulates the concept of cultural positioning bias, making it easy to understand and appreciate the scope of the problem.
*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The CULTURELENS benchmark is well-designed and comprehensive, allowing for systematic evaluation across various cultures and models. The quantitative metrics provide a solid basis for measuring and comparing bias levels.
*   **Effective Mitigation Strategies:** The proposed mitigation methods, particularly the MFA framework, demonstrate promising results in reducing cultural positioning bias.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments involve a diverse set of LLMs and ablation studies to evaluate the effectiveness of different mitigation strategies.
*   **Well-written and Organized:** The paper is well-written, easy to follow, and presents its arguments in a clear and logical manner.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Cultures:** While the benchmark includes 10 cultures, this represents a small fraction of the world's diverse cultures. Expanding the benchmark to include a wider range of cultural contexts would further strengthen the findings.
*   **Automated Evaluation:** The reliance on an LLM judge for classifying "insider" vs. "outsider" perspectives could introduce its own biases. Validating these classifications with human annotators at scale would improve the robustness of the evaluation. The Cohen's Kappa is on the lower end.
*   **Complexity of MFA Framework:** The MFA framework, while effective, is relatively complex and computationally intensive. Exploring simpler and more efficient mitigation strategies would be beneficial.

**Potential Influence on the Field:**

This paper has the potential to influence the field by:

*   Raising awareness about the importance of considering the implicit cultural lens of LLMs.
*   Providing a concrete benchmark and methodology for evaluating cultural positioning bias.
*   Inspiring further research on developing more effective and efficient mitigation strategies.
*   Promoting the development of more equitable and inclusive AI systems that are sensitive to cultural diversity.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty of the problem definition, the systematic methodology, the effectiveness of the proposed mitigation strategies, and the potential influence on the field, a score of 8 is warranted. While the paper has some limitations in terms of the scope of cultures and reliance on automated evaluation, its contributions are significant and warrant recognition. The problem of cultural positioning bias is important, and this paper offers a solid framework for understanding and addressing it.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Vision Transformers: the threat of realistic adversarial patches
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21084v1)
- **Authors**: Kasper Cools, Clara Maathuis, Alexander M. van Oers, Claudia S. Hübner, Nikos Deligiannis, Marijke Vandewal, Geert De Cubber
- **Abstract**: The increasing reliance on machine learning systems has made their security a critical concern. Evasion attacks enable adversaries to manipulate the decision-making processes of AI systems, potentially causing security breaches or misclassification of targets. Vision Transformers (ViTs) have gained significant traction in modern machine learning due to increased 1) performance compared to Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs) and 2) robustness against adversarial perturbations. However, ViTs remain vulnerable to evasion attacks, particularly to adversarial patches, unique patterns designed to manipulate AI classification systems. These vulnerabilities are investigated by designing realistic adversarial patches to cause misclassification in person vs. non-person classification tasks using the Creases Transformation (CT) technique, which adds subtle geometric distortions similar to those occurring naturally when wearing clothing. This study investigates the transferability of adversarial attack techniques used in CNNs when applied to ViT classification models. Experimental evaluation across four fine-tuned ViT models on a binary person classification task reveals significant vulnerability variations: attack success rates ranged from 40.04% (google/vit-base-patch16-224-in21k) to 99.97% (facebook/dino-vitb16), with google/vit-base-patch16-224 achieving 66.40% and facebook/dinov3-vitb16 reaching 65.17%. These results confirm the cross-architectural transferability of adversarial patches from CNNs to ViTs, with pre-training dataset scale and methodology strongly influencing model resilience to adversarial attacks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the vulnerability of Vision Transformers (ViTs) to realistic adversarial patches in person vs. non-person classification tasks, motivated by security applications.  It adapts a Creases Transformation (CT) technique from CNN-based adversarial patch generation to create more physically plausible adversarial patches. The study examines the transferability of these CNN-derived patches to fine-tuned ViT models with different pre-training strategies. Experiments show significant variations in vulnerability across ViT models, influenced by pre-training dataset size and methodology. The results confirm the cross-architectural transferability of adversarial patches and highlight the need for robust defense mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting the Creases Transformation technique, previously used on CNNs, to generate more realistic adversarial patches for ViTs.  While adversarial patches are not a new concept, the investigation into *realistic* patch attacks on *ViTs* specifically, and the assessment of the vulnerability variation across ViT models pre-trained with *different* data scales and methodologies, is novel. The focus on *cross-architecture* transferability from CNNs to ViTs is also a significant contribution.
* **Significance:** The paper is significant because it addresses a critical security concern: the vulnerability of ViTs, increasingly used in security-sensitive applications, to adversarial attacks. Demonstrating the effectiveness of transferable adversarial patches, especially realistic ones, is crucial for understanding the limitations of these models and for motivating the development of effective defenses. The investigation into the impact of different pre-training strategies on adversarial robustness is particularly valuable for guiding the development of more secure ViT models. This research helps to inform development for robust architecture-agnostic defense mechanisms for use in the critical security sectors.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of adversarial attacks on ViTs in security-critical applications.
    * **Methodological Rigor:**  The adaptation of the DAP framework and the use of the Creases Transformation add a level of realism to the adversarial patches.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The study comprehensively evaluates the vulnerability of several ViT models with different pre-training strategies.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The focus on realistic, printable patches makes the findings practically relevant for real-world security deployments.
    * **Reproducibility:** The paper clearly specifies hyperparameter values during training, as well as random seed usage to ensure reproducibility.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Defense:**  While the paper highlights the vulnerability, it does not propose or evaluate specific defense mechanisms in detail. It suggests data augmentation with shape noise as a possible mitigation, but doesn't test this hypothesis.
    * **Patch Size Optimization:** While the paper specifies pixel square patches during initialisation and placement, it does not address patch size optimisation, as this could have resulted in different attack success rates.
    * **Dataset Size:** While the custom binary classification dataset used was created with COCO's object categories (person vs. non-person), the subset of available person classification datasets were limited in their size. More comprehensive data set sizes could be considered in future work.
* **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings are likely to influence future research in adversarial robustness of ViTs.  It could stimulate the development of ViT-specific defense mechanisms, architecture-agnostic defense mechanisms, and adversarial training strategies. It could also influence the choice of pre-training strategies for developing more robust ViT models.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by demonstrating the vulnerability of ViTs to realistic adversarial patches and exploring the impact of pre-training strategies on adversarial robustness. It is well-motivated, methodologically sound, and practically relevant. However, the lack of concrete defense proposals and analysis limits its overall impact. Therefore, a score of 7 is justified: it is a solid contribution with useful insights but could be improved by addressing the limitations mentioned above.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### UniTransfer: Video Concept Transfer via Progressive Spatial and Timestep Decomposition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21086v1)
- **Authors**: Guojun Lei, Rong Zhang, Chi Wang, Tianhang Liu, Hong Li, Zhiyuan Ma, Weiwei Xu
- **Abstract**: We propose a novel architecture UniTransfer, which introduces both spatial and diffusion timestep decomposition in a progressive paradigm, achieving precise and controllable video concept transfer. Specifically, in terms of spatial decomposition, we decouple videos into three key components: the foreground subject, the background, and the motion flow. Building upon this decomposed formulation, we further introduce a dual-to-single-stream DiT-based architecture for supporting fine-grained control over different components in the videos. We also introduce a self-supervised pretraining strategy based on random masking to enhance the decomposed representation learning from large-scale unlabeled video data. Inspired by the Chain-of-Thought reasoning paradigm, we further revisit the denoising diffusion process and propose a Chain-of-Prompt (CoP) mechanism to achieve the timestep decomposition. We decompose the denoising process into three stages of different granularity and leverage large language models (LLMs) for stage-specific instructions to guide the generation progressively. We also curate an animal-centric video dataset called OpenAnimal to facilitate the advancement and benchmarking of research in video concept transfer. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method achieves high-quality and controllable video concept transfer across diverse reference images and scenes, surpassing existing baselines in both visual fidelity and editability. Web Page: https://yu-shaonian.github.io/UniTransfer-Web/
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces UniTransfer, a novel framework for video concept transfer that emphasizes precise control and high-quality results.  The core idea is a progressive spatial and timestep decomposition strategy. Spatially, the video is broken down into foreground, background, and motion components.  Temporally, the denoising diffusion process is decomposed into coarse, medium, and fine-grained stages, each guided by prompts generated by a Large Language Model (LLM). A self-supervised pretraining strategy is used to learn disentangled representations from unlabeled video data. The framework is implemented using a Diffusion Transformer (DiT) architecture.  The authors also curate a new animal-centric video dataset, OpenAnimal, for benchmarking video concept transfer.  Experiments demonstrate the method's superiority over existing baselines in terms of visual fidelity and editability across various tasks, including character, garment, background, and motion transfer.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the combination of spatial and temporal decomposition within a diffusion-based video generation framework. While spatial decomposition has been explored before (e.g., in MIMO), the integration of a *progressive* timestep decomposition guided by LLM-generated prompts is a key innovation. The use of self-supervised pretraining to aid in disentangled representation learning is also a strong point. However, individual elements such as DiT-based architecture and individual datasets are by themselves non-novel. The random masking pre-training may be regarded as a clever modification of existing techniques.

*   **Significance:** Video concept transfer is a practically relevant and challenging problem. UniTransfer tackles the limitations of previous methods by offering more granular control and improved quality.  The OpenAnimal dataset is a valuable contribution, as it addresses the lack of animal-centric video data for training and benchmarking. The results presented demonstrate significant improvements over baselines, suggesting that UniTransfer has the potential to advance the state-of-the-art in video editing. Moreover, the modularity of the method is a benefit for generalization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled Approach:** The spatial and temporal decomposition strategy provides a well-structured way to control video generation.
    *   **LLM Integration:** The use of LLMs for generating hierarchical prompts is a creative way to leverage the reasoning capabilities of LLMs to guide the denoising process.
    *   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The experiments cover a wide range of video concept transfer tasks and datasets, demonstrating the versatility of the framework.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:**  OpenAnimal fills a gap in the availability of animal-centric video data.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The multi-stage process is computationally demanding. The method relies on several pre-trained models (segmentation, optical flow, LLM), introducing additional dependencies and complexity. Although it's a general solution, each individual part needs to be working seamlessly.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:** While the improvements over baselines are significant, the core building blocks (DiT, diffusion models) are well-established. The novelty is in the *combination* and *adaptation* of these elements.
    *   **Artifacts:** the limitations mentioned by the authors are to be addressed, i.e., imperfect separation of foregrounds, it remains a real limitation of the present work.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in video concept transfer and video editing. The spatial and temporal decomposition strategy could be adopted by other researchers, and the OpenAnimal dataset could become a standard benchmark. Also, it may create better video editing products for the film/advertising industry.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty and significance of the paper, I would assign it a score of **7**. The core idea of progressive spatial and timestep decomposition is novel and addresses a critical limitation in video concept transfer. The use of LLMs to guide the denoising process is also a strong point. The method shows compelling results. However, the method also relies on several pre-trained models and each is expected to work perfectly. The paper uses established DiT-based architecture, as well as other techniques that is a modification of past ideas and so it can't be put in a higher position.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Are Modern Speech Enhancement Systems Vulnerable to Adversarial Attacks?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2509.21087v1)
- **Authors**: Rostislav Makarov, Lea Schönherr, Timo Gerkmann
- **Abstract**: Machine learning approaches for speech enhancement are becoming increasingly expressive, enabling ever more powerful modifications of input signals. In this paper, we demonstrate that this expressiveness introduces a vulnerability: advanced speech enhancement models can be susceptible to adversarial attacks. Specifically, we show that adversarial noise, carefully crafted and psychoacoustically masked by the original input, can be injected such that the enhanced speech output conveys an entirely different semantic meaning. We experimentally verify that contemporary predictive speech enhancement models can indeed be manipulated in this way. Furthermore, we highlight that diffusion models with stochastic samplers exhibit inherent robustness to such adversarial attacks by design.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the vulnerability of modern speech enhancement (SE) systems, specifically predictive and generative models, to adversarial attacks. The authors demonstrate that adversarial noise, crafted to be psychoacoustically masked by the original input, can be injected to manipulate the enhanced speech output, causing it to convey an entirely different semantic meaning. The study experimentally verifies the susceptibility of contemporary predictive SE models to these attacks.  It also highlights that diffusion models, particularly those with stochastic samplers, inherently exhibit robustness against such adversarial manipulations due to their design.  The paper proposes an attack loss function and optimization procedure, and analyzes the differences in vulnerability across different SE model types, including the effect of stochastic sampling.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of applying adversarial attacks to *speech enhancement* systems with the intent of changing the *semantic meaning* of the output is a novel application.
